# Crisis in Venezuela

This article is about the socioeconomic and political crisis in Venezuela. For other uses, see [Venezuelan crisis (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_crisis_\(disambiguation\)).

| Casualties and losses |  |
| 374+ deaths in protests[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53] 12,620+ detentions[^cite_note-54][^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56] 9,100,000+ displaced as of 17 May 2025[^cite_note-57] |  |

An ongoing socioeconomic and political crisis began in [Venezuela](32374.md) during the presidencies of [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez), [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md) and [Delcy Rodríguez](44199228.md).[^cite_note-58] It has been marked by [hyperinflation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperinflation), starvation,[^cite_note-59] disease, crime, and mortality rates, resulting in [massive emigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_refugee_crisis).[^cite_note-Larmer-1-11-18-60] Food shortages and hyperinflation have ended, but inflation remains high. It is the worst economic crisis in Venezuela's history and considered by some sources as more severe than the [Great Depression in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression_in_the_United_States), the [1985–1994 Brazilian economic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985%E2%80%931994_Brazilian_economic_crisis), or the 2008–2009 [hyperinflation in Zimbabwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperinflation_in_Zimbabwe).[^cite_note-Kurmanaev-2019-61] In 2019, the [Institute of International Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_International_Finance) called it the single largest economic collapse outside of war in 45 years.[^cite_note-Kurmanaev-2019-61] Writers have compared aspects, such as [unemployment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment) and GDP contraction, to [Bosnia and Herzegovina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Bosnia_and_Herzegovina) after the 1992–95 [Bosnian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_War), and those in [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Russian_financial_crisis), [Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Period) and [Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Albanian_civil_unrest) following the [Revolutions of 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1989).[^cite_note-WartimeEconomy3-62][^cite_note-NYRoB-20183-63]

In 2010, Chávez declared an "economic war" due to increasing [shortages in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_in_Venezuela). The crisis intensified under the Maduro government, growing more severe as a result of [low oil prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010s_oil_glut),[^cite_note-Mariana_Zuñiga_and_Anthony_Faiola-64] and a drop in oil production from lack of maintenance and investment.[^cite_note-Larmer-1-11-18-60] In 2016, the opposition-led National Assembly declared a "health humanitarian crisis."[^cite_note-ANDeclara-65] The government failed to cut spending in the face of falling oil revenues, denying the existence of a crisis,[^cite_note-el-nacional.com-66] and violently [repressed opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_repression).[^cite_note-Larmer-1-11-18-60][^cite_note-SecondDay3-67] [Extrajudicial killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killings) by the government became common, with the UN reporting 5,287 killings by the [Special Action Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Action_Forces) in 2017, with another 1,569 killings in the first half of 2019, stating some were "a reprisal for participation in anti-government demonstrations."[^cite_note-nytimes.com-68]

The [European Union](9317.md), the [Lima Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lima_Group), the US and other countries have applied sanctions against officials and members of the military and security forces, as a response to human rights abuses, degradation of the rule of law, and corruption.[^cite_note-sanctions2-69] In 2017, the US extended its sanctions. These blocked oil exports, paralyzed the Venezuelan economy, froze Venezuelan financial assets and blocked the country's access to international payments systems.[^cite_note-70] According to a 2019 report by [Mark Weisbrot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Weisbrot) and [Jeffrey Sachs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Sachs), sanctions caused Venezuela a loss of \$38 billion between 2016-19, prevented the government from resolving the crisis using fiscal and monetary policy changes, and in combination with preexisting negative trends resulted in an estimated 40,000 excess deaths between 2017-18.[^cite_note-Weisbrot-2019-71] The report's methodology and conclusions were criticized by other economists.[^cite_note-Hausmann\:0-72][^cite_note-\:1-73]

Supporters of Chávez and Maduro said the problems result from an "economic war" on Venezuela[^cite_note-GDPShrink2-74] falling oil prices, [international sanctions](60408367.md),[^cite_note-75] and the business elite.[^cite_note-76] Most observers cite anti-democratic governance,[^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78] [political corruption](23453241.md), [authoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism), [human rights violations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_violations), high dependence on oil,[^cite_note-B\&C20113-79][^cite_note-economia.uol3-80][^cite_note-81] and gross mismanagement of the economy as causes.[^cite_note-economia.uol3-80][^cite_note-82] Others attribute the crisis to the "[socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism_of_the_21st_century)",[^cite_note-83][^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86] "[populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populist)",[^cite_note-87][^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89][^cite_note-90] or "hyper-populist" nature of the government's policies,[^cite_note-91] and the use of these to maintain political power.[^cite_note-FPmarch20133-92][^cite_note-STRATexpensive2-93][^cite_note-Chosun_Ilbo_populism2-94]

The crisis has affected the life of the average Venezuelan at all levels. By 2017, hunger had escalated to the point where almost 75% of the population had lost an average of 9 kg (over 19 lbs),[^cite_note-96] and more than half did not have enough income to meet basic food needs.[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-Reuters29Mar3-98][^cite_note-Reuters-99] 20% of Venezuelans (5.4 million) had left the country by 2021,[^cite_note-Venezuela_situation-100][^cite_note-MHaug193-101] and 7.7 million had emigrated by 2024.[^cite_note-102] In 2019, 25% of Venezuelans needed some form of humanitarian assistance.[^cite_note-UNODC3-103] 95% of the population by 2021 was living in poverty, and 77% lived under extreme poverty, the highest ever recorded in the country.[^cite_note-Caracas_Chronicles-2021-104] Following increased sanctions throughout 2019, the Maduro government abandoned policies established by Chávez such as price and currency controls, which resulted in the country seeing a temporary rebound from economic decline before [COVID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Venezuela).[^cite_note-Venezuela's_timid_gains_in_taming_i-105][^cite_note-106] By 2019, as a response to the devaluation of the official [bolívar currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_bol%C3%ADvar), the population increasingly relied on US dollars for transactions.[^cite_note-Maduro_says_'thank_God'_for_dollari-107] In 2022, after the implementation of mild [economic liberalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalization), poverty decreased slightly and the economy grew for the first time in 8 years. The reforms intensified inequality, with Venezuela reaching the highest level of inequality in the Americas.[^cite_note-108]

The [United States Intervention in Venezuela](82021123.md) on 3 January 2026 resulted in the capture of Maduro and the assumption of the Venezeulan presidency by Delcy Rodríguez. According to *[The New York Times](30680.md)*, Venezuela has been a *de facto* [puppet state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puppet_state) since the intervention, with the US State Department under Marco Rubio exercising control over core aspects of Venezuela's governance, including its domestic finances, government appointments, revenues, foreign policy, and the distribution of its natural resources.[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-\:6-110][^cite_note-Callaghan_01042026-111] Venezuela's quarterly GDP growth rate of 2.5% in the immediate aftermath of the attack was its lowest in five years.[^cite_note-112]

## History

See also: [History of Venezuela (1999–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Venezuela_\(1999%E2%80%93present\))

| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez_series)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez_series?action=edit\&redlink=1)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez_series) |

After [attempting a coup d'état in 1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Venezuelan_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempts) and being pardoned by President [Rafael Caldera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Caldera),[^cite_note-113] [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) was elected president and maintained the presidency from 1999 until his death in 2013. After increasing oil prices in the early 2000s provided additional funds to Venezuela, Chávez established [Bolivarian missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_missions), aimed at providing public services to improve economic, cultural, and social conditions.[^cite_note-PAHO54-114][^cite_note-http-115][^cite_note-Barreiro_C-116][^cite_note-117] The Missions entailed the construction of thousands of free medical clinics for the poor,[^cite_note-PAHO54-114] and the enactment of food[^cite_note-Barreiro_C-116] and housing subsidies.[^cite_note-http-115] A 2010 [OAS](22788.md) report[^cite_note-118] indicated achievements in addressing illiteracy, healthcare and poverty,[^cite_note-IACHRRequests-119] and economic and social advances.[^cite_note-120] Quality of life for Venezuelans had also improved.[^cite_note-UN-121] While poverty declined more than 20 percent between 2002 and 2008, "aid was disbursed to *some* of the poor...in a way that ended up helping the president and his allies and cronies more than anyone else", according to Corrales and Penfold.[^cite_note-122] [Teresa A. Meade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teresa_A._Meade) wrote that Chávez's popularity strongly depended "on the lower classes who have benefited from these health initiatives and similar policies."[^cite_note-123]

The social works initiated by Chávez's government relied on oil exports. The nation's richness in natural resources [hindered its industrial development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_disease) and diversification.[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-FPmarch20133-92][^cite_note-USVEN-126][^cite_note-127] By the early 2010s, economic actions taken by Chávez's government during the preceding decade, such as overspending[^cite_note-NPRdec2014-128][^cite_note-ELPAISfeb2015-129][^cite_note-FPmarch2013-124][^cite_note-130] and price controls,[^cite_note-Barreiro_C-116][^cite_note-ByNfood-131] became unsustainable. Venezuela's economy faltered while [poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty),[^cite_note-UN-121][^cite_note-FPpoverty-132] [inflation](38286.md)[^cite_note-133] and shortages increased. On 2 June 2010, Chávez declared an "economic war" due to increasing [shortages in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_in_Venezuela).[^cite_note-ECONwarCHAVEZ-1]



&#x20;The world oil price had fallen to a historic low when [Chavez was elected in December 1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Venezuelan_presidential_election) and rose over 1000% over the first ten years of his presidency. Major crisis events occur after the 2008 oil price collapse and ensuing period of volatility.&#x20;

### Maduro presidency

| This article is part of [a series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro) about[Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md) |
| --- |
| Personal [Early life](7161476.md#Early_and_personal_life) [Early career](7161476.md#Early_career) [Madurismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madurismo) [Cilia Flores](9530834.md) (wife) [Nicolás Maduro Guerra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_Guerra) (son)   [53rd President of Venezuela](51287559.md) [Venezuelan crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_crisis) [Economic policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_administration) [International trips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_international_presidential_trips_made_by_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro) [2018 Attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Caracas_drone_attack) [Human rights](#Human_rights) [Presidential crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_presidential_crisis) [2017 Constituent Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Constituent_National_Assembly_of_Venezuela) [Operation Tun Tun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Tun_Tun) [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Venezuela) [Protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests_against_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_and_Delcy_Rodr%C3%ADguez) [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Venezuelan_protests) [2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_protests) [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Venezuelan_protests) [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Venezuelan_protests)   Elections [2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Venezuelan_presidential_election) [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Venezuelan_presidential_election) [2024](60825396.md)   [Deposition and prosecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Absolute_Resolve) [2026 US intervention](82021123.md) [US trial and prosecution](82024138.md) [Cartel of the Suns allegations](45272133.md) [Tren de Aragua ties allegations](70859925.md#United_States)   [Nicolás Maduro's signature](7161476.md)  Seal of the President of Venezuela |
| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_series)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_series)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_series) |



[Diosdado Cabello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diosdado_Cabello) beside [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md) and his wife, [Cilia Flores](9530834.md)

Following Chávez's death in 2013, [Nicolás Maduro](7161476.md) became president after defeating his opponent [Henrique Capriles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henrique_Capriles) by 235,000 votes, a 1.5% margin.[^cite_note-134] Maduro continued most of the existing economic policies of Chávez. Upon entering the presidency, his administration faced a high inflation rate and large shortages of goods,[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-qz.com-136][^cite_note-economist.com-137] problems left over from Chávez's policies.[^cite_note-FPmarch2013-124][^cite_note-USVEN-126][^cite_note-NPRdec2014-128][^cite_note-ELPAISfeb2015-129][^cite_note-unstable-138]

Maduro said capitalist [speculation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speculation) had driven high rates of inflation and created widespread shortages of basic necessities. In September 2013, the government seized a toilet paper factory, saying that its owners had been hoarding goods in hopes of selling them later at a higher price.[^cite_note-139] In November, the government seized an electronics store over allegations of price gouging, and imposed price controls on another. The imposition of price controls on certain industries led to a surge in demand for those goods. Though the price controls made goods more affordable for those who could obtain them, they could not meet overall demand.[^cite_note-140] Maduro announced a three phase plan to alleviate the country's economic troubles. The goals of his "economic offensive" were to activate domestic production, break with "oil rentism", guarantee supply and fix prices at affordable levels.[^cite_note-141] In 2014, The National Assembly granted him greater powers to impose price controls and seize businesses he accused of [price gouging](879011.md) scarce goods.[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143]

By 2014, Venezuela had entered an [economic recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_recession). The economy contracted by 4.8%, 4.9% and 2.3% in the first three quarters of the year. 12-month inflation reached 63.6%. Oil exports fell by 14.2%. Despite poor GDP growth, some social indicators continued to improve. Extreme poverty was reduced to 5.4% and unemployment fell to 5.9%.[^cite_note-144] The crisis intensified as a result of [oil oversupply in early 2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010s_oil_glut),[^cite_note-Mariana_Zuñiga_and_Anthony_Faiola-64] and a drop in Venezuela's oil production from lack of maintenance and investment.[^cite_note-Larmer-1-11-18-60] Between 2014 and 2016, real GDP declined by an estimated 24.3%.[^cite_note-Weisbrot-2019-71] By 2016, the country had an inflation rate of 800%, the highest in its history.[^cite_note-Reuters2016Inflation-145][^cite_note-FPblackbox-146]

In January 2016, the National Assembly declared a "health humanitarian crisis" given the "serious shortage of medicines, medical supplies and deterioration of humanitarian infrastructure", asking Maduro's government to "guarantee immediate access to the list of essential medicines that are basic and indispensable and that must be accessible at all times."[^cite_note-ANDeclara-65] In August, [Secretary-General of the United Nations](162415.md) [Ban Ki-moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ban_Ki-moon) declared that there was a humanitarian crisis in Venezuela caused by the lack of basic needs, including food, water, sanitation and clothing.[^cite_note-147]

Extrajudicial killings by the Venezuelan government became common, with the [United Nations](31769.md) (UN) reporting 5,287 killings by the [Special Action Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Action_Forces) (FAES) in 2017, with at least another 1,569 killings recorded in the first six months of 2019. On the grounds that many of these killings constituted extrajudicial executions, the Venezuelan authorities responded with security operations aimed at "neutralizing, repressing and criminalizing political opponents and people critical of the government". The UN stated that the Special Action Forces "would plant arms and drugs and fire their weapons against the walls or in the air to suggest a confrontation and to show the victim had resisted authority" and that some of the killings were "done as a reprisal for \[the victims'] participation in anti-government demonstrations."[^cite_note-nytimes.com-68]

The government failed to cut spending in the face of falling oil revenues, and has dealt with the crisis by denying its existence[^cite_note-el-nacional.com-66] and violently repressing opposition.[^cite_note-Larmer-1-11-18-60][^cite_note-SecondDay3-67]

Before the [2019 presidential crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_presidential_crisis), the Maduro government denied several offers of aid, stating that there was not a humanitarian crisis and that such claims were used to justify foreign intervention.[^cite_note-Bundle1-148] Maduro's refusal of aid worsened the effects of Venezuela's crisis.[^cite_note-Bundle1-148]

In March 2019, *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* said that "Mr. Maduro has long used food and other government handouts to pressure impoverished Venezuelans to attend pro-government rallies and to support him during elections as the country's economic meltdown has intensified."[^cite_note-AidEffort-149] In 2019, *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* wrote that the Maduro government had obtained "extra money from selling gold (both from illegal mines and from its reserves) and narcotics".[^cite_note-150]

#### Elections and protests

Further information: [Venezuelan protests (2014–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_protests_\(2014%E2%80%93present\)), [2017 Venezuelan Constituent Assembly election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_Constituent_Assembly_election), [2018 Venezuelan presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Venezuelan_presidential_election), and [Venezuelan presidential crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_presidential_crisis)



Venezuelans demonstrating during the [2016 Venezuelan protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_2016_Venezuelan_protests)



[Juan Guaidó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Guaid%C3%B3) during the [2019 Venezuelan uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Venezuelan_uprising_attempt)

As a result of discontent with the government, in the [2015 parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Venezuelan_parliamentary_election) the opposition was elected to the majority in the [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_\(Venezuela\)),[^cite_note-NYTmuzzle-151] after which the outgoing ([lame duck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lame_duck_\(politics\))) National Assembly—consisting of [Bolivarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarianism) officials—filled the [Supreme Tribunal of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_\(Venezuela\)), the highest court in Venezuela, with Maduro allies.[^cite_note-NYTmuzzle-151][^cite_note-152]

Maduro disavowed the National Assembly in 2017 leading to the [2017 Venezuelan constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_constitutional_crisis);[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] as of 2018, some considered the National Assembly the only "legitimate" institution left in the country,[^cite_note-161] and human rights organizations said there were no independent institutional checks on presidential power.[^cite_note-166] [Protests grew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_protests) to their most "combative" since they began in 2014.[^cite_note-167] On 1 May 2017, Maduro called for a [constituent assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Assembly) that would draft a new constitution to replace the [1999 Venezuela Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Venezuela_Constitution).[^cite_note-USAmay-168] The members of the Constituent Assembly would not be elected in open elections, but selected from social organizations loyal to Maduro.[^cite_note-USAmay-168] It would also allow him to stay in power during the [interregnum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interregnum) and skip the [2018 presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Venezuelan_presidential_election).[^cite_note-169]

Many countries considered these actions a bid by Maduro to stay in power indefinitely,[^cite_note-suspended-170] and over 40 countries, along with NGOs, stated that they would not recognize the [2017 Constituent National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Constituent_National_Assembly) (ANC).[^cite_note-40nos-171][^cite_note-VoteRigging-172][^cite_note-EU1-173][^cite_note-BLOOLmerc-174][^cite_note-175] The [Democratic Unity Roundtable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Unity_Roundtable)—the opposition to the incumbent ruling party—boycotted the election,[^cite_note-176] and the incumbent [Great Patriotic Pole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Patriotic_Pole), dominated by the [United Socialist Party of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Socialist_Party_of_Venezuela), won almost all seats in the assembly by default.[^cite_note-177]

The ANC was sworn in on 4 August 2017,[^cite_note-178] and the next day declared itself to be the government branch with supreme power in Venezuela, banning the opposition-led National Assembly from performing actions that would interfere with the assembly.[^cite_note-179]

In February 2018 Maduro called presidential elections, four months before the prescribed date.[^cite_note-BBCweighs-180] There were many irregularities, including the banning from standing of several major opposition parties. Maduro was declared the winner in May 2018. Many said the elections were invalid.[^cite_note-181] Politicians both internally and internationally said Maduro was not legitimately elected,[^cite_note-182] and considered him an ineffective dictator.[^cite_note-183] In the months leading up to his 10 January 2019 inauguration, Maduro was facing pressure to step down by nations and bodies including the [Lima Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lima_Group) (excluding Mexico), the United States, and the OAS; this pressure was increased after the new National Assembly of Venezuela was sworn in on 5 January 2019.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-SecondTerm-185]

The [2019 presidential crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Venezuelan_presidential_crisis) came to a head when the National Assembly stated that the results of the May 2018 presidential election were invalid and declared National Assembly president [Juan Guaidó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Guaid%C3%B3) to be the acting president, citing several clauses of the 1999 [Venezuelan Constitution](1211684.md).[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187]

#### 2024 Venezuelan political crisis

Main article: [2024 Venezuelan political crisis](77669383.md)

The [2024 Venezuelan political crisis](77669383.md) is the crisis that continued after the [2024 Venezuelan presidential election](60825396.md) results were announced.[^cite_note-FacesSetback-188][^cite_note-Kurmanaev-NYT-6-8-2024-189][^cite_note-DragsOn-190] Maduro ran for a third consecutive term, while former diplomat [Edmundo González Urrutia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmundo_Gonz%C3%A1lez_Urrutia) represented the [Unitary Platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_Platform) ([Spanish](26825.md): *Plataforma Unitaria Democrática*; PUD), the main opposition political alliance, after the [Venezuelan](32374.md) government barred leading candidate [María Corina Machado](5791389.md) from participating.[^cite_note-MCM-191][^cite_note-192]

Academics, news outlets and the opposition provided "strong evidence" according to *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)*[^cite_note-GuardianEvidenceShows-193] to suggest that González won the election by a wide margin.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-BBC2Aug-195] The government-controlled [National Electoral Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Electoral_Council_\(Venezuela\)) (CNE) announced results claiming a narrow Maduro victory on 29 July.[^cite_note-OAS_report_VE_pres_elec_DECO-196] A 6 August article in *The New York Times* stated that the CNE declaration that Maduro won "plunged Venezuela into a political crisis that has claimed at least 22 lives in violent demonstrations, led to the jailing of more than 2,000 people and provoked global denunciation."[^cite_note-Kurmanaev-NYT-6-8-2024-189] In the [aftermath](#Aftermath) of the government's announcement of falsified results, [protests broke out across the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Venezuelan_protests), as the Maduro administration initiated [Operation Tun Tun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Tun_Tun), a crackdown on dissent, and [detained opposition political figures](#Charges_and_arrests_of_other_politicians_and_journalists) while refusing to relinquish power. Criminalization of protest was widely condemned by human rights organizations.[^cite_note-AICondemnViolence-197] Maduro did not acknowledge the results which showed him losing the election,[^cite_note-GuardianRefusal-198] and instead asked the [Supreme Tribunal of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_\(Venezuela\)) (TSJ) on 1 August to audit and approve the results.[^cite_note-ReutersCourtRatifies-199][^cite_note-APCourtCertifies-200][^cite_note-CriesFoul-201][^cite_note-BBCStayinPower-202] On 22 August, as anticipated,[^cite_note-BBCStayinPower-202][^cite_note-LoyaltoMaduro-203][^cite_note-HandPicked-204] the TSJ described the CNE's statement of Maduro winning the election as "validated".[^cite_note-ReutersCourtRatifies-199]

## Basic needs

### Poverty

*The Wall Street Journal* reported in March 2019 that poverty was double that of 2014.[^cite_note-9Charts-205] A study from [Andrés Bello Catholic University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Bello_Catholic_University) indicated that by 2019 at least 8 million Venezuelans did not have enough to eat.[^cite_note-9Charts-205] A UN report estimated in March 2019 that 94% of Venezuelans live in poverty, and that one quarter of Venezuelans need some form of [humanitarian assistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanitarian_assistance).[^cite_note-Reuters29Mar-206]

According to the Living Conditions Survey by the Andrés Bello Catholic University (Encovi in Spanish, *Encuesta de Condiciones de Vida*), by 2021 94.5% of the population was in poverty based on income, out of which 76.6% lived under extreme poverty, the highest figure ever recorded in the country.[^cite_note-Caracas_Chronicles-2021-104]

### Food and water

More than 70% of Venezuela's food is imported;[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207] Venezuela became overly dependent on food imports which the country could no longer afford when the price of oil dropped in 2014. Chávez gave the military control of food, and nationalized much of the industry, which was then neglected, leading to production shortages. With a "diminished food supply", Maduro put "generals in charge of everything from butter to rice".[^cite_note-MilitaryFood-208] With the military in charge of food, food trafficking became profitable, bribes and corruption common, and food did not reach the needy. The government imports most of the food the country needs, it is controlled by the military, and the price paid for food is higher than justified by market prices. Venezuelans were spending "all day waiting in lines" to buy [rationed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationed) food, "pediatric wards filled up with underweight children, and formerly middle-class adults began picking through rubbish bins for scraps".[^cite_note-MilitaryFood-208]



A group of Venezuelans eating garbage on the streets of [Caracas](55289.md) in May 2018

Several other factors have led to shortages: imports over the two years until the end of 2017 declined by two-thirds; hyperinflation has made food too costly for many Venezuelans; and for those who depend on food boxes supplied by the government, "these do not reach all Venezuelans who need them, provision of boxes is intermittent, and receipt is often linked to political support of the government".[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207]

Corruption became a problem in the distribution of food. The operations director at one food import business says "he pays off a long roster of military officials for each shipment of food he brings in from ... the US. It's an unbroken chain of bribery from when your ship comes in until the food is driven out in trucks."[^cite_note-MilitaryFood-208] A [National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_National_Guard) [lieutenant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant) denies this charge, saying corruption would be worse if the military were not involved; government and military officials say the opposition is overstating the corruption problem for their own benefit.[^cite_note-MilitaryFood-208] Retired General Antonio Rivero said that "Maduro is trying to prevent soldiers from going hungry and being tempted to participate in an uprising against an increasingly unpopular government", adding that using the military to control food distribution has "drained the feeling of rebellion from the armed forces" by giving soldiers access to food denied others, with the resulting corruption increasing shortages for the general public.[^cite_note-MilitaryFood-208]

The *[colectivos](42254804.md)* are also involved in food trafficking, selling food on the black market; a *colectivo* leader told [InSight Crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/InSight_Crime) that trafficking food and medicine is as profitable as drug-running, but carries less risk.[^cite_note-Devolution-209] With shadowy connections to the government, *The Washington Post* says "some have been put in charge of the distribution of government food packages in poor areas—giving them control over hungry neighborhoods."[^cite_note-Enforcers-210]

The Associated Press reports that people gather every evening in downtown Caracas in search of food thrown out on a sidewalk; the people are typically unemployed, but are "frequently joined by small business owners, college students and pensioners—people who consider themselves middle class even though their living standards have long ago been pulverized by triple-digit inflation, food shortages and a collapsing currency".[^cite_note-APGarbage-211] A waste collection official in Maracaibo reported that most of the trash bags he received had been gone through by people searching for food.[^cite_note-Flamingos-212] One dump reports finding parts of dismembered animals, like "dogs, cats, donkeys, horses and pigeons" and there is evidence that people are eating wildlife such as anteaters, flamingos, vultures and lizards.[^cite_note-Flamingos-212]

"Hunger, malnutrition, and severe food shortages are widespread in all Venezuela", according to Human Rights Watch.[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213] Doctors at 21 public hospitals in 17 Venezuelan states told *[The New York Times](30680.md)* in 2017 that "their emergency rooms were being overwhelmed by children with severe malnutrition—a condition they had rarely encountered before the economic crisis began", and that "hundreds have died". The government has responded with "a near-total blackout of health statistics, and by creating a culture in which doctors are often afraid to register cases and deaths that may be associated with the government's failures".[^cite_note-KOHUT-214]

The [Food and Agriculture Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_and_Agriculture_Organization) of the UN said that less than 5% of Venezuelans were undernourished between 2008 and 2013, but that number had more than doubled, to almost 12% from 2015 and 2017, representing 3.7 million people.[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213] A 2016 survey found that almost three-quarters of the population said that, because of improper nutrition, they had lost on average 8.7 kg (19.4 lbs)[^cite_note-UPI75-95] and 64% said they lost 11 kg (24 lbs) in 2017.[^cite_note-WeightLoss-215][^cite_note-216] A 2016 Venebarometro poll of 1,200 Venezuelans found almost half are no longer able to eat three daily meals; the government blames this on an "economic war" they say is waged by the opposition.[^cite_note-APGarbage-211]

A UN report said that because of lack of water and sanitation, 4.3 million Venezuelans needed assistance in 2019.[^cite_note-Reuters29Mar-206]

During the [2019 Venezuelan blackouts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Venezuelan_blackouts) which started on 7 March, the water distribution system also had shortages.[^cite_note-DailyStruggle-217] Analysts said that two-thirds of Venezuela's population (20 million people) were without water, partially or completely, in the weeks after the blackouts.[^cite_note-DailyStruggle-217][^cite_note-SwarmSewage-218][^cite_note-SeekingWater-219][^cite_note-220] The head of the infectious disease department at the [University Hospital of Caracas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital_Cl%C3%ADnico_Universitario), María Eugenia Landaeta said that, without access to clean water, the chance of people contracting bacterial infections increased, and that doctors had seen during the blackouts "surges in diarrhea, [typhoid fever](31596.md) and [hepatitis A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hepatitis_A)",[^cite_note-DailyStruggle-217] while non-sterile water and lack of hygiene was contributing to [postpartum infections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postpartum_infections).[^cite_note-DailyStruggle-217]

By 2017, hunger had escalated to the point where almost seventy-five percent of the population had lost an average of over 8 kg (over 19 lbs) in weight[^cite_note-221] and more than half did not have enough income to meet their basic food needs.[^cite_note-222] An UN report estimated in March 2019 that 94% of Venezuelans lived in poverty,[^cite_note-Reuters29Mar-206][^cite_note-Reuters-99] and by 2021 almost twenty percent of Venezuelans (5.4 million) had left their country.[^cite_note-Venezuela_situation-100][^cite_note-MHaug193-101] The UN analysis estimates in 2019 that 25% of Venezuelans need some form of humanitarian assistance. Venezuela led the world in [murder rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_intentional_homicide_rate), with 81.4 per 100,000 people killed in 2018, making it the third most violent country in the world.[^cite_note-UNODC-223] Following increased international sanctions throughout 2019, the Maduro government abandoned policies established by Chávez such as price and currency controls, which resulted in the country seeing a temporary rebound from economic decline before [COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Venezuela) entered Venezuela the following year.[^cite_note-Venezuela's_timid_gains_in_taming_i-105][^cite_note-224] As a response to the devaluation of the official [bolívar currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_bol%C3%ADvar), by 2019 the population increasingly started relying on US dollars for transactions. Maduro described dollarization as an "escape valve" that helps the recovery of the country, the spread of productive forces in the country and the economy. However, Maduro said that the Venezuelan bolívar remained as the national currency.[^cite_note-Maduro_says_'thank_God'_for_dollari-107]

### Health care

Main article: [Health care in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_in_Venezuela)



Healthcare spending by percentage of Venezuela's GDP

[^cite_note-225]

During the Bolivarian Revolution, the government began providing free healthcare, with Cuban medical professionals providing aid. The government's failure to concentrate on healthcare and a reduction in spending on healthcare, along with unchecked government corruption resulted in avoidable deaths due to severe shortages of medical supplies and equipment, and the emigration of medical professionals to other countries.[^cite_note-BGapril2015-226][^cite_note-FPchavezcare-227]

Venezuela's reliance on imported goods and the complicated exchange rates initiated under Chávez led to increasing shortages during the late 2000s and into the 2010s that affected the availability of medicines and medical equipment in the country.[^cite_note-FPchavezcare-227] Associated Press says the government stopped publishing medical statistics in 2010.[^cite_note-APdoc-228] The Health Minister changed multiple times during Chávez's presidency. According to a high-ranking official of Venezuela's Health Ministry, the ministers were treated as scapegoats whenever issues with public health arose in Venezuela.[^cite_note-FPchavezcare-227] He also said that officials of the Health Ministry engaged in corruption to enrich themselves by selling goods intended for public healthcare to others.[^cite_note-FPchavezcare-227]

Early in the Maduro presidency, the government could not supply enough money for medical supplies among healthcare providers, with the president of the Venezuelan Medical Federation saying that 9 of 10 large hospitals had only 7% of required supplies and private doctors reporting numbers of patients that are "impossible" to count "dying from easily treatable illnesses when Venezuela's downward economic slide accelerated after Chávez's death".[^cite_note-APdoc-228] Many Venezuelans died avoidable deaths with medical professionals having scarce resources and using methods that were replaced decades ago.[^cite_note-BGapril2015-226] In February 2014, doctors at the University of Caracas Medical Hospital stopped performing surgeries due to the lack of supplies, even though nearly 3,000 people required surgery.[^cite_note-229]

By early 2015, only 35% of hospital beds were available and 50% of operating rooms could not function due to the lack of resources.[^cite_note-BGapril2015-226][^cite_note-FPchavezcare-227] In March 2015, a Venezuelan NGO, Red de Medicos por la Salud, reported that there was a 68% shortage of surgical supplies and a 70% shortage of medicines in Venezuelan pharmacies.[^cite_note-FPchavezcare-227] In 2018, the [Pan American Health Organization](1246706.md) (PAHO) reported that approximately one-third (22,000 of 66,138) of registered physicians left Venezuela as of 2014.[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207] [Rosemary DiCarlo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosemary_DiCarlo) from the UN said that 40% of medical professionals had left Venezuela and supplies of medicine were at 20% of levels needed.[^cite_note-MortalitySoared-230] The Venezuelan Medical Federation said that doctors were leaving the public health care system because of shortages of drugs and equipment and poor pay. In August 2015 [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) said "We have rarely seen access to essential medicines deteriorate as quickly as it has in Venezuela except in war zones."[^cite_note-TheTimes-231] In 2015, the government reported that a third of patients admitted to public hospitals died.[^cite_note-NBC1in3-232] The medications of individuals who die are re-distributed through small-scale and local efforts, with the help of the families of the deceased, to try to supply surviving patients.[^cite_note-233]

One study of 6,500 households by three of the main universities in Venezuela found that "74% of the population had lost on average nineteen pounds in 2016".[^cite_note-NYRoB-20183-63] In April 2017 Venezuela's health ministry reported that maternal mortality jumped by 65% in 2016 and that the number of infant deaths rose by 30%.[^cite_note-234] It also said that the number of cases of malaria was up by 76%.[^cite_note-235] Shortly after Minister of Health Antonieta Caporale released in 2017 this data, and health statistics showing increases in 2016 infant and maternal mortality and infectious diseases, Maduro fired her and replaced the physician with a pharmacist close to vice-president [Tareck El Aissami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tareck_El_Aissami), Luis López Chejade.[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207][^cite_note-236] The publications were removed from the Ministry's website, and no further health data has been made available, although the government had produced health bulletins for several decades.[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207]

In March 2019, *The Wall Street Journal* reported that the "collapse of Venezuela's health system, once one of the best in Latin America, has led to a surge in infant and maternal mortality rates and a return of rare diseases that were considered all but eradicated. Health officials say malaria, yellow fever, diphtheria, dengue and tuberculosis are now spreading from Venezuela to neighboring countries as Venezuelan refugees surge over borders."[^cite_note-AidEffort-149] The United Nations estimated in 2019 that 2.8 million Venezuelans have healthcare needs, 300,000 are at risk of dying with cancer, diabetes or HIV as they have not had access to medicine for more than a year, and preventable diseases like diphtheria, malaria, measles and tuberculosis are rising in 2019, along with hepatitis A, because of sanitation and lack of access to water.[^cite_note-Reuters29Mar-206] The April 2019 HRW/Johns Hopkins report showed this rise in infectious and preventable diseases, as well as increasing malnutrition, infant and maternal death, and undertreatment of [HIV](14170.md).[^cite_note-GuardianHRW-237] Inflation and medicine shortages have meant that patients are asked to bring their own food, water and soap, and medical supplies including scalpels and syringes.[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207][^cite_note-NPRHRW-238] In August 2019, as part of regional efforts to help Venezuelan migrants, the United States promised that it will provide thousands of doses of HIV medication to prevent the spread of HIV/AIDS and to treat those who have it.[^cite_note-239]

#### 2019 Human Rights Watch/Johns Hopkins report



Venezuelans [protesting in 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_protests) for medicine due to the [shortages in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_in_Venezuela)

In April 2019, Human Rights Watch (HRW) and [Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johns_Hopkins_Bloomberg_School_of_Public_Health) published the results of a joint, year-long research project in a report entitled "Venezuela's humanitarian emergency: Large-scale UN response needed to address health and food crises".[^cite_note-GuardianHRW-237][^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213] Combined with data from the [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO), the PAHO[^cite_note-GuardianHRW-237] and Venezuelan sources,[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213] the report was based on 156 interviews[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207] with Venezuelan emigrants to Colombia and Brazil, officials from relief and humanitarian organizations, Venezuelan health care professionals,[^cite_note-WAPOHRW-240] and UN and government officials from Brazil and Colombia.[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213] Most of the interviews occurred in July or August 2018 in visits by the experts to the Venezuelan border towns of [Cúcuta](623811.md), Colombia and [Boa Vista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boa_Vista,_Roraima) or [Pacaraima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacaraima), Brazil.[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207]

*The Washington Post* stated that the HRW/Johns Hopkins report "paints an extremely grim picture of life in Venezuela, whose once-prosperous economy has imploded because of mismanagement and corruption under Maduro";[^cite_note-WAPOHRW-240] it documents rising maternal and infant death, spread of preventable diseases, food insecurity, and child malnutrition.[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213] HRW declared that the "combination of severe medicine and food shortages ... with the spread of disease ... amounts to a complex humanitarian emergency that requires a full-scale response by the United Nations secretary-general."[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213] *The Washington Post* states that the report describes a healthcare system that is in "utter collapse", with diseases that are preventable via vaccination spreading, and "dramatic surges" in infectious diseases once eradicated in Venezuela.[^cite_note-WAPOHRW-240]

The economic crisis in Venezuela started around 2010, and the health crisis followed by two years and significantly worsened in 2017, but the situation in 2019 "is even more dismal than researchers expected".[^cite_note-NPRHRW-238] Paul Spiegel, MD, who was the editor and reviewer of the report said, "Venezuela is a middle-income country with a previously strong infrastructure, so just to see this incredible decline ... in such a short period of time is quite astonishing."[^cite_note-NPRHRW-238] [Alberto Paniz-Mondolfi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Paniz-Mondolfi), a doctor in [Barquisimeto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barquisimeto), Venezuela who is a member of the Venezuelan National Academy of Medicine, told NPR that the report gave an accurate, thorough and timely depiction of the medical situation in his country; he had no affiliation with the report, but said that he had seen cases where there were not even [catheters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catheter) for hooking up children who appeared to have malnutrition for [intravenous therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intravenous_therapy).[^cite_note-NPRHRW-238] Spiegel adds that, because of the infrastructure and trained personnel in Venezuela, aid can be distributed quickly once delivered to Venezuela.[^cite_note-NPRHRW-238]

Maduro administration response

Maduro administration on humanitarian crisis

> On May 16, 2018, President Maduro said that "everything \[that has been said] about measles and diphtheria is a lie, we vaccinate the whole community for free" and that "with regards to food, Venezuela has unique policies, which have enabled us to carry on with a program allowing us to maintain levels of food that are necessary for the people." Days later, the undersecretary of health, Indhriana Parada, gave a speech at WHO highlighting the "achievements" of the Venezuelan health system. She said that "in Venezuela there is no humanitarian crisis" and that "Venezuela guarantees access to basic medicines to the most vulnerable groups through distribution policies." In the case of malaria, she said that government measures had "reduced incidence by 50 percent".\
>
>
> ---

— "Venezuela's humanitarian emergency: Large-scale UN response needed to address health and food crises".

[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207]

The Maduro administration does not publish health statistics,[^cite_note-APdoc-228][^cite_note-NPRHRW-238] but rather it "paint\[s] a rosy picture of its health care system".[^cite_note-NPRHRW-238] *The Guardian* reported Maduro's response to the country's health care crisis as "inadequate".[^cite_note-GuardianHRW-237] "Because of the intransigence of President Nicolás Maduro—who has blamed deprivations on US sanctions and refused to allow anything beyond a trickle of assistance to enter the country"—aid has not been delivered quickly enough.[^cite_note-WAPOHRW-240] Reuters reported that "Maduro says there is no crisis and no need for humanitarian aid, blaming U.S. sanctions for the country's economic problems."[^cite_note-ReutersHRW-241] Venezuela's foreign minister [Jorge Arreaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Arreaza) did not respond to a letter asking for Venezuela's "views regarding the extent of the crisis and the policies it was implementing to address it" before the HRW/Johns Hopkins report was published.[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207]

The HRW summary of the HRW/Johns Hopkins report said, "The Venezuelan authorities during the presidency of Nicolás Maduro have proven unable to stem the crisis, and have in fact exacerbated it through their efforts to suppress information about the scale and urgency of the problems."[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213] The Associated Press said Maduro "suppress\[es] information" and has made the problem worse.[^cite_note-APHRW-242] The Americas director for HRW, José Miguel Vivanco said, "Venezuelan authorities publicly minimise and suppress information about the crisis, and harass and retaliate against those who collect data or speak out about it, while also doing far too little to alleviate it."[^cite_note-GuardianHRW-237] The report discusses a teaching physician who said "residents are threatened with being expelled from the program or their hospital if they include a malnutrition diagnosis in medical records", causing malnutrition to be understated in Venezuelan data.[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207]

The report states that "many analysts have argued that the government's own policies have played a role in causing the economic crisis ... However, under the presidency of Nicolás Maduro, the Venezuelan government has denied the crisis, hidden health statistics and data, harassed health professionals who speak out about the reality on the ground, and made it harder for sufficient humanitarian assistance to reach the Venezuelan people. Through these policies and practices, authorities have contributed to the worsening humanitarian crisis documented in this report."[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207] The [International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Covenant_on_Economic,_Social_and_Cultural_Rights) (ICESCR) is a multilateral treaty ratified by Venezuela; it commits its parties to "the enjoyment of the highest attainable standard of physical and mental health", and the right to an adequate "standard of living" and "adequate food".[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207] The Constitution of Venezuela provides for the [right to health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_health).[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207] The HRW/Johns Hopkins report states that, facing deteriorating health conditions, the government's suppression of information and actions against those speaking about the crisis "represent a violation of Venezuela's obligations to respect, protect, and fulfill the right to health" to which Venezuelans are entitled from both the ICESCR treaty and their Constitution.[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207]

Following the April HRW/Johns Hopkins report, and amid announcements from the United Nations about the scale of the humanitarian crisis, along with increasing international pressure, Maduro met with the Red Cross, and it announced it would triple its budget for aid to Venezuela.[^cite_note-MaduroSoftens-245] The increased aid would focus in four areas: the migration crisis, the health care system collapse, water and sanitation, and prisons and detention centers.[^cite_note-MaduroSoftens-245] Maduro, for the first time, indicated he was prepared to accept international aid—although denying a humanitarian crisis exists.[^cite_note-246] *The Wall Street Journal* said that the acceptance of humanitarian shipments by Maduro was his first acknowledgement that Venezuela is "suffering from an economic collapse",[^cite_note-AidEffort-149] and *The Guardian* reported that Maduro's stance has softened in the face of increasing pressure.[^cite_note-MaduroSoftens-245] Guaidó said the acceptance of humanitarian aid was the "result of our pressure and insistence",[^cite_note-AidEffort-149] and called on Venezuelans to "stay vigilant to make sure incoming aid is not diverted for 'corrupt' purposes".[^cite_note-WarnsAgainst-247]

#### Infectious and preventable diseases



From less than 36,000 cases in 2009, to 414,000 cases of malaria in Venezuela in 2017

[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213]

In 1961, Venezuela was the first country declared free of [malaria](20423.md).[^cite_note-Finnegan,_William-2016-248] In 2009, the WHO reported there were less than 36,000 cases of malaria in Venezuela.[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213] In 2013, Venezuela registered a new high in the number of cases of malaria in the past 50 years, and by 2014, was the only country in Latin America where the incidence of malaria was increasing, allegedly in part due to illegal mining;[^cite_note-249] and medical shortages in the country which hampered treatment.[^cite_note-250] By 2016, Venezuela's malaria-prevention program had collapsed, and there were more than a hundred thousand cases of malaria yearly.[^cite_note-Finnegan,_William-2016-248] In 2017, there were 414,000 confirmed cases of malaria, according to the WHO.[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213][^cite_note-NPRHRW-238]

Other preventable diseases that "were rare or nonexistent before the economic crisis, have surged",[^cite_note-NPRHRW-238] including [diphtheria](58937.md), [measles](58911.md), and [tuberculosis](30653.md). "Venezuela did not experience a single case of diphtheria between 2006 and 2015"; according to the HRW/Johns Hopkins report, since mid-2016, 1,500 of the 2,500 suspected cases have been confirmed.[^cite_note-WAPOHRW-240][^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213] Between 2008 and 2015, there was one recorded case of measles, in 2012; since June 2017, 6,200 of the 9,300 reported cases have been confirmed.[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213][^cite_note-WAPOHRW-240] The highest rate of tuberculosis in four decades was reached in 2017.[^cite_note-NPRHRW-238] In 2014, there were 6,000 reported cases of tuberculosis; preliminary data shows more than 13,000 for 2017.[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213]

In 2014, shortages of [antiretroviral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antiviral) medicines to treat [HIV/AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS) affected about 50,000 Venezuelans, potentially causing thousands of Venezuelans with HIV to develop AIDS.[^cite_note-FOXlatino2014-251] In 2018, PAHO estimated that 90% of Venezuelans who had HIV and were registered by the government—69,308 of the 79,467 registered[^cite_note-NPRHRW-238]—were not receiving antiretroviral treatment.[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213][^cite_note-WAPOHRW-240] The PAHO report estimated that in six years, new HIV cases grew by 24% through 2016, after which the government stopped providing data.[^cite_note-NPRHRW-238] NPR reported: "New HIV infections and AIDS-related deaths have increased sharply, in large part because the vast majority of HIV-positive Venezuelans no longer have access to antiretroviral medications."[^cite_note-NPRHRW-238] Because of a shortage of HIV test kits, there may be more people who have HIV but are not aware.[^cite_note-NPRHRW-238] The HRW/Johns Hopkins report says: "Venezuela is the only country in the world where large numbers of individuals living with HIV have been forced to discontinue their treatment as a result of the lack of availability of antiretroviral (ARV) medicines."[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207]

In late 2014, Venezuelans began saying that due to shortages of medicines, it was hard to find [acetaminophen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acetaminophen) to help alleviate symptoms of the newly introduced [chikungunya](366112.md) virus, a potentially lethal mosquito-borne disease.[^cite_note-WSJdisease2014-252] In September 2014, the Venezuelan government stated that 400 Venezuelans were infected with chikungunya;[^cite_note-ELPAIS2014-253] the [Central University of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_University_of_Venezuela) stated that there could be between 65,000 and 117,000 Venezuelans infected.[^cite_note-ScienceCHIK-254] In August 2015 independent health monitors said that there were more than two million people infected with chikungunya while the government said there were 36,000 cases.[^cite_note-TheTimes-231]

##### COVID-19 pandemic

Main article: [COVID-19 pandemic in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Venezuela)

As a result of the COVID-19 pandemic, which reached Venezuela in March 2020, costs for services such as internet and telephone lines rose between 80% and 749%,[^cite_note-255] further limiting access to these utilities. Shortages of beds and essential medical equipment, such as latex gloves and antibiotics, have severely limited the capabilities of the country's medical infrastructure.[^cite_note-256][^cite_note-cfr.org-257]

In April 2020 the Venezuelan government asked the [Bank of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_of_England) to sell \$US1.02 billion of the Venezuelan gold reserves held by the bank to help the government fund its response to the COVID-19 pandemic. This was followed on 14 May by a legal claim by the [Venezuelan Central Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Bank_of_Venezuela) (BCV) asking the Bank of England to send the proceeds of the sale of gold to the [United Nations Development Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Development_Programme). The claim stated that the funds would then be used to buy healthcare equipment, medicine, and food to address the country's "COVID-19 emergency".[^cite_note-258] The [UK Foreign Office](59653.md) had previously agreed to a request from the [Trump administration](52231341.md) to block the release of Venezuela's gold.[^cite_note-guardian051020-259] In July 2020, the [UK High Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UK_High_Court) ruled that the gold could not be released to the BCV because the UK government recognised Juan Guaidó as the "constitutional interim president of Venezuela". However, in October 2020, an appeals court overturned the High Court decision and asked the UK Foreign Office to clarify who it recognised as president of Venezuela. *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* wrote that the position of the UK government was unclear as it "maintains full consular and diplomatic relations with the Venezuela government".[^cite_note-guardian051020-259]  In early 2026 following the [Capture of Nicolas Maduro](82021123.md), a report in the Guardian claimed the UK holds at least \$1.95bn (£1.4bn).[^cite_note-260]

#### Women, maternal and infant

In 2016, [infant mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality) increased 30% in one year, to 11,466 deaths of children under the age of one.[^cite_note-Infant2016-261][^cite_note-NPRHRW-238] By 2019, the UN reported that infant mortality had "soared".[^cite_note-MortalitySoared-230] "Venezuela is the only South American country where infant mortality has returned to levels last seen in the 1990s", according to the HRW/Johns Hopkins report.[^cite_note-HRWFullScale-213] [Maternal mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternal_mortality) also increased 65% in one year, to 756 deaths.[^cite_note-NPRHRW-238][^cite_note-Infant2016-261]

[Abortion](765.md) is illegal in Venezuela;[^cite_note-ABCesAborto-262] the director of a large family planning clinic in Venezuela indicated that more women are arranging for permanent sterilization, and that more are presenting with "complications from clandestine abortions".[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207] One of the causes, according to the Venezuelan Association for Alternative Sexual Education, is the severe shortage of oral and injectable contraceptives and [intrauterine devices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intrauterine_device).[^cite_note-ABCesAborto-262]

The HRW/Johns Hopkins report states that the more than 454,000 Venezuelan women who have emigrated to Colombia face 'threats of sexual exploitation and abuse, trafficking, and sexual and reproductive rights violations"; violence based on gender accounted for more than 12% of 2018 health-care events, and indigenous women may be at higher risk.[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207]

Venezuelan women emigrating are at risk for becoming sex trafficking targets virtually anywhere they flee to. Cases of trafficking in Peru, the United States, Spain, and Colombia display the highest numbers.[^cite_note-263]

##### Pregnancy and motherhood

Due to lack of medical supplies, food and medical care in Venezuelan hospitals, many pregnant women in Venezuela are crossing the border into neighboring countries to give birth.[^cite_note-264] Lack of basic medicine and equipment is causing preventable deaths and maternity is a very high risk for women, especially since there are no blood banks in the event of excessive bleeding.[^cite_note-Collapses-265] Hospitals frequently have water and electricity outages and only 7% of emergency services are fully operative.[^cite_note-Crumbles-266] Maternal mortality is estimated to have increased by 65% from 2013 to 2016, and unsafe abortions have contributed to 20% of preventable maternal deaths.[^cite_note-Collapses-265] According to Amnesty International, causes of the increase in maternal deaths include a lack of medical personnel and supplies like anticoagulants, scar healing cream, painkillers, antibiotics, antiseptics, and other tools and equipment.[^cite_note-267]

##### Statelessness

Cúcuta, a city on the Colombian-Venezuela border, has received 14,000 Venezuelan patients at the Erasmo Meoz University Hospital since 2016 and is expecting to receive even more.[^cite_note-Crumbles-266] In this hospital, 75% of the newborns born in the first two months of the year 2019 were Venezuelans.[^cite_note-bloomberg.com-268] The situation has strained the budget of these hospitals, putting Erasmo Meoz 14 million dollars into debt.[^cite_note-bloomberg.com-268] While Colombia is the most impacted since it shares a border, women are also traveling to Brazil to give birth.[^cite_note-reuters.com-269] The number of births of Venezuelan babies attended to in [Boa Vista, Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boa_Vista,_Roraima), has increased from 700 in 2014 to 50,000 in 2017.[^cite_note-reuters.com-269] Venezuelan mothers have also been fleeing to neighboring Peru and Ecuador.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271] For Colombian citizenship it is required that Colombian citizens be born to at least one Colombian parent or be born to foreign parents who meet residence requirements and are eligible to become citizens.[^cite_note-272] Due to the influx of Venezuelan babies being born in Colombia and the Venezuelan government's inability to issue citizenship, Colombia has introduced a new measure that will give these Colombian-born newborns Colombian citizenship to avoid 'statelessness'.[^cite_note-bloomberg.com-268] The measure went into effect August 2019 and includes babies of Venezuelan parents born in Colombia starting in January 2015, having given citizenship to approximately 27,000 babies born in Colombia over the past four years.[^cite_note-bloomberg.com-268]

#### Mental health

Following the Bolivarian Revolution, the rate of suicide among Venezuelans quadrupled over two decades.[^cite_note-Orozco-2019-27] As a result of the crisis, stressors resulting in suicide included economic burden, hunger and loneliness due to the emigration of relatives.[^cite_note-Orozco-2019-27]

In 2015, concerns about shortages and inflation overtook violent crime as Venezuelans' main worry for the first time in years according to pollster Datanálisis. The chief executive of Datanálisis, Luis Vicente Leon, said that Venezuelans had greater concerns over shortages and became preoccupied with the difficulties surrounding them instead. [Eldar Shafir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eldar_Shafir), author and American behavioral scientist, said that the psychological "obsession" with finding scarce goods in Venezuela is because the rarity of the item makes it "precious".[^cite_note-BBCprecious-273]

In 2016, reporters from *The New York Times* visited six psychiatric wards across the Venezuela at the invitation of doctors; all reported shortages of medicine and even food. In the investigation, they reported that El Pampero Hospital had not employed a psychiatrist in two years, and that it only had running water for only a few hours a day. The hospital, the article said, also suffered from shortages of basic personal-care and cleaning supplies, such as soap, shampoo, toothpaste or toilet paper. The nurses declared that without sedatives, they had to restrain patients or lock them in isolation cells to keep them from harming themselves. The reporters also noted that the government had denied that its public hospitals were suffering from shortages, and had refused multiple offers of international medical aid.[^cite_note-274]

Despite the threat of violent protests, the economic crisis affected children more than violence. Abel Saraiba, a psychologist with children's rights organization Cecodap said in 2017, "We have children from a very early age who are having to think about how to survive", with half of her young clients requiring treatment because of the crisis. Children are often forced to stand in food lines or beg with their parents, while the games they play with other children revolve around finding food.[^cite_note-275] Friends of the Child Foundation psychologist Ninoska Zambrano said that children are offering sexual services for food. Zambrano said "Families are doing things that not only lead them to break physically, but in general, socially, we are being morally broken".[^cite_note-276]

In 2017, suicide increased by 67% among the elderly and 18% among minors; by 2018, reports emerged of a rapidly increasing suicide rate due to the stressors surrounding the crisis.[^cite_note-277]

#### Medical care and elections

[Mission Barrio Adentro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mission_Barrio_Adentro) was a program established by Chávez to bring medical care to poor neighborhoods; it was staffed by Cubans that were sent to Venezuela in exchange for petroleum. *The New York Times* interviewed sixteen Cuban medical professionals in 2019 who had worked for *Barrio Adentro* prior to the 2018 Venezuelan presidential elections; all sixteen revealed that they were required to participate in voting fraud.[^cite_note-Unspeakable-278] Some of the Cubans said that "command centers" for elections were placed near clinics to facilitate "dispatching doctors to pressure residents".[^cite_note-Unspeakable-278] Some tactics reported by the Cubans were unrelated to their profession: they were given counterfeit cards to vote even though they were not eligible voters, they witnessed vote tampering when officials opening ballot boxes and destroyed ballots, and they were told to instruct easily manipulated elderly patients in how to vote.[^cite_note-Unspeakable-278]

But they also "described a system of deliberate political manipulation"; their services as medical professionals "were wielded to secure votes for the governing Socialist Party, often through coercion", they told *The New York Times*.[^cite_note-Unspeakable-278] Facing a shortage of supplies and medicine, they were instructed to withhold treatment–even for emergencies–so supplies and treatment could be "doled out closer to the election, part of a national strategy to compel patients to vote for the government".[^cite_note-Unspeakable-278] They reported that life-saving treatment was denied to patients who supported the opposition. As the election neared, they were sent door-to-door, on house visits with a political purpose: "to hand out medicine and enlist voters for Venezuela's Socialist Party".[^cite_note-Unspeakable-278] Patients were warned that they could lose their medical care if they did not vote for the socialist party, and that, if Maduro lost, ties would be broken with Cuba, and Venezuelans would lose all medical care. Patients with chronic conditions, at risk of death if they couldn't get medicine, were a particular focus of these tactics. One said that government officials were posing as doctors to make these house calls before elections; 'We, the doctors, were asked to give our extra robes to people. The fake doctors were even giving out medicines, without knowing what they were or how to use them," he said.[^cite_note-Unspeakable-278]

### Housing



Slums in Caracas seen above El Paraíso tunnel

Since the mid-2000s during Chávez's presidency, Venezuela has had a housing crisis.[^cite_note-nyt-279] In 2005, the Venezuelan Construction Chamber (CVC) estimated that there was a shortage of 1.6 million homes, with only 10,000 of 120,000 promised homes constructed by Chávez's government despite billions of dollars in investments.[^cite_note-WP2005-280] Poor Venezuelans attempted to construct homes on their own despite structural risks.[^cite_note-WP2005-280]

By 2011, there was a housing shortage of 2 million homes, with nearly twenty prime developments being occupied by squatters following Chávez's call for the poor to occupy "unused land".[^cite_note-nyt-279][^cite_note-REUTjune2011-281] Up to 2011, only 500,000 homes were constructed during the Chávez administration, with over two-thirds of the new housing developments being built by private companies; his government provided about the same amount of housing as previous administrations.[^cite_note-REUTjune2011-281] Housing shortages were further exacerbated when private construction halted due to the fear of property expropriations and because of the government's inability to construct and provide housing.[^cite_note-nyt-279] Urban theorist and author Mike Davis said in July 2011 to *The Guardian*, "Despite official rhetoric, the Bolivarianist regime has undertaken no serious redistribution of wealth in the cities and oil revenues pay for too many other programmes and subsidies to leave room for new housing construction."[^cite_note-282] By 2012, a shortage of building materials also disrupted construction, with metal production at a 16-year low.[^cite_note-LATIN2014-283] By the end of Chávez's presidency in 2013, the number of Venezuelans in inadequate housing had grown to 3 million.[^cite_note-LATIN2014-283]

Under the Maduro government, housing shortages continued to worsen. Maduro announced in 2014 that due to the shortage of steel, abandoned cars and other vehicles would be acquired by the government and melted to provide rebar for housing.[^cite_note-LATIN2014-283] In April 2014, Maduro ruled by decree that Venezuelans who owned three or more rental properties would be forced by the government to sell their rental units at a set price or they would face fines or have their property possessed by the government.[^cite_note-284] By 2016, residents of government-provided housing, who were usually supporters of the government, began protesting due to the lack of utilities and food.[^cite_note-285]

## Social

### Corruption

Main article: [Corruption in Venezuela](23453241.md)

Corruption is high in Venezuela according to the [Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International) [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index) and is prevalent at many levels of society.[^cite_note-B\&C2011-286] While corruption is difficult to measure reliably, in 2018 Transparency International ranked Venezuela among the top 13 most corrupt countries out of 180 measured, tied with [Iraq](42043567.md), but ahead of [Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Afghanistan), [Burundi](21490998.md), [Equatorial Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Equatorial_Guinea), [Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Guinea), [North Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_North_Korea), [Libya](17633.md), [Somalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Somalia), [South Sudan](48114847.md), [Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Sudan), Syria and [Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Yemen).[^cite_note-287] A 2016 poll found that 73% of Venezuelans believed their police were corrupt.[^cite_note-288] [Latinobarómetro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latinobar%C3%B3metro)'s 2018 report said that 65% of Venezuelans believed their president was involved in corruption, and 64% believed that government officials were corrupt.[^cite_note-289][^cite_note-290] Discontent with corruption was cited by opposition-aligned groups as one of the reasons for the [2014 Venezuelan protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Venezuelan_protests).[^cite_note-291] A once wealthy country, Venezuela's economy was driven into political and [economic crisis](2878852.md) by corruption and mismanagement.[^cite_note-292]

### Crime

Further information: [Crime in Venezuela](37476256.md) and [Colectivo (Venezuela)](42254804.md)



Murder rate (murder per 100,000 citizens) from 1998 to 2018. \
Sources: OVV,

[^cite_note-293]

[^cite_note-294]

&#x20;PROVEA,

[^cite_note-UNODC2011-295]

[^cite_note-UNODC2014-296]

&#x20;UN;

[^cite_note-UNODC2011-295]

[^cite_note-UNODC2014-296]

[^cite_note-AFP2012-297]

&#x20;UN data is extrapolated between 2007 and 2012 because of missing data

Escalating violent crime, especially murder, had been called "perhaps the biggest concern" of Venezuelans during the crisis.[^cite_note-298] Venezuela had "by various measures, the world's highest violent-crime rate" in 2017, and almost none of crimes that were reported were prosecuted.[^cite_note-Finnegan,_William-2016-248] InSight Crime says the crisis has "all too often been obscured by the government's reluctance to release damning crime statistics".[^cite_note-KidnapDouble-299] *The New Yorker* reporter found that even stairwells in a public hospital were not safe from robbers, who preyed on staff and patients despite the large number of security forces guarding the hospital, saying this was because the police were assigned to contain journalists who might embarrass the government with exposés on the state of the hospital; they were not assigned to protect its occupants. The police allegedly collaborated with the robbers receiving a cut of what they stole.[^cite_note-Finnegan,_William-2016-248]

According to The U.S. Bureau of Diplomatic Security, street gang violence, "corrupt" underpaid police officers, "an inefficient and politicized judicial system", an extremely troubled prison system, and an increased widespread of weaponry has resulted in the majority of criminal activity in Venezuela, with murder being the crime committed the most.[^cite_note-OSAC2018-300] The bureau states that there were 73 daily violent deaths in 2018, and that the government "often attempts to refute or repudiate reports of increasing crime and murder rates; however, independent observers widely reject" the Venezuelan government's claims.[^cite_note-OSAC2018-300] The government says there were 60 daily homicides in 2016, and 45 daily in 2015,[^cite_note-HerreraNephew-301] corresponding with Venezuela's downward economic spiral; the OVV says the numbers are higher.[^cite_note-HerreraNephew-301]

For 2015, the government says the rate of homicides was 70.1 per 100,000 people.[^cite_note-HerreraNephew-301] The Venezuelan Observatory of Violence (OVV) says the rate was 91.8 homicides per 100,000 people (in 2015, the comparative U.S. number was 4.9 per 100,000 inhabitants).[^cite_note-HerreraNephew-301] According to the [World Bank](36752.md), the 2016 homicide rate was 56 per 100,000, making Venezuela third in the world, after El Salvador and Honduras.[^cite_note-MurderDips-302] OVV data has 23,047 homicides committed in Venezuela in 2018, a rate of 81.4 per 100,000 people, with the decline being attributed to emigration.[^cite_note-MurderDips-302]

&#x20;According to the *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times)*,

> ... carjack gangs set up ambushes, sometimes laying down nail-embedded strips to puncture tires of vehicles ferrying potential quarry. Motorists speak matter-of-factly of spotting body parts along roadways. ... While most crime victims are poor, they also include members of the middle and upper classes and scores of police and military personnel killed each year, sometimes for their weapons. ... "Before the thieves would only rob you," is a common refrain here in the capital. "Now they kill you."[^cite_note-303]

Venezuela led the world in [murder rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_intentional_homicide_rate), with 81 per 100,000 people killed in 2018; the third most violent country.[^cite_note-UNODC3-103]

As a response to the high rate of crime, the Venezuelan government banned private ownership of firearms by some individuals in 2012.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306] *[El País](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Pa%C3%ADs)* reported in 2014 that Chávez had years earlier assigned *colectivos* to be "the armed wing of the Bolivarian Revolution" for the Venezuelan government, giving them weapons, communication systems, motorcycles and surveillance equipment to exercise control in the hills of Caracas where police are forbidden entry.[^cite_note-ELPAIScolectivo-307] In 2006, they received arms and funding from the state when they were brought under the government's community councils.[^cite_note-Devolution-209] Chávez eliminated the Metropolitan Police in 2011, turning security over to the colectivos in some Caracas barrios.[^cite_note-Devolution-209] Some weapons given to the groups include [assault rifles](18950865.md), [submachine guns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Submachine_gun) and [grenades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grenades).[^cite_note-ELPAIScolectivo-307] Despite the Venezuelan government's statements saying that only official authorities can carry weapons for the defense of Venezuela, *colectivos* are armed with automatic rifles such as [AK-47s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AK-47), submachine guns, fragmentation grenades, and tear gas.[^cite_note-ISCnov2014-308][^cite_note-ABCmarch2013-309]



Number of kidnappings in Venezuela 1989–2011.\
Source: [CICPC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuerpo_de_Investigaciones_Cient%C3%ADficas,_Penales_y_Criminal%C3%ADsticas)
