[^cite_note-310]

[^cite_note-CICPC2009-311]

[^cite_note-312]

\
&#x20;Note: [Express kidnappings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Express_kidnapping) may not be included in data.

During the 2014 Venezuelan protests against Maduro, *colectivos* acted against the opposition protesters.[^cite_note-lapatilla.com-313] As the crisis intensified, armed gangs have taken control of cities.[^cite_note-Kurmanaev-2019-61] The Civil Association for Citizen Control said that more than half of those killed during the protests were killed by *colectivos*.[^cite_note-314] Human Rights Watch described colectivos as "armed gangs who use violence with impunity" to harass political opponents of the Venezuelan government.[^cite_note-ReutersCOLECTIVO-315][^cite_note-316] [Amnesty International](18947898.md) calls them "armed pro-government supporters who are tolerated or supported by the authorities".[^cite_note-317] During the 2019 Venezuelan blackouts in March, Maduro called on the armed paramilitary gangs, saying, "The time has come for active resistance".[^cite_note-Demonic-318] As blackouts continued, on 31 March, citizens protested the lack of electricity and water in Caracas and other cities; Maduro called again on the colectivos, asking them "to defend the peace of every barrio, of every block".[^cite_note-ArmedGroups-319] Videos circulated on social media showing colectivos threatening protesters and shooting in the streets;[^cite_note-WPSunday-320] two protestors were shot.[^cite_note-ArmedGroups-319]

There is no reliable data on kidnapping in Venezuela and available data is considered an underestimate;[^cite_note-KidnapDouble-299][^cite_note-HerreraNephew-301] it is against the law to pay ransom, and according to criminologists, at least 80% of kidnappings are not reported for fear of retaliation,[^cite_note-OSAC2018-300] or because relatives prefer to negotiate, hoping the hostage will be released and fearing they will be killed if authorities are contacted.[^cite_note-KidnapDouble-299][^cite_note-HerreraNephew-301] Available data underestimates the amount of [express kidnapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Express_kidnap), where victims are typically released in less than two days after relatives pay a quick ransom.[^cite_note-KidnapDouble-299][^cite_note-HerreraNephew-301] Most express kidnapping victims are released, but in 2016, 18 were killed.[^cite_note-KidnapDouble-299] At least 80% of kidnappings occur in a limited area around Caracas and including [Miranda State](556284.md). In the areas where most kidnappings occur, the government set up so-called "peace zones" where official police withdrew and gangs took over;[^cite_note-KidnapDouble-299] "experts say the government has armed these groups ... \[who] ... control large territories, financed through extortion and the drug trade".[^cite_note-HerreraNephew-301]

Illegal mining creates pools of standing water that encourage mosquito breeding, a partial cause of the increase in malaria seen in Venezuela.[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207]

The murder rate in Venezuela had also decreased significantly between 2017 and 2019. In 2018, Venezuela's murder rate–described as the highest in the world–had begun to decrease to 81.4 per 100,000 people according to the Venezuelan Violence Observatory (OVV), with the organization stating that this downward trend was due to the millions of Venezuelans that emigrated from the country at the time.[^cite_note-321] The murder rate declined even further to 60.3 in 2019.[^cite_note-322]

### Human rights

Further information: [Human rights in Venezuela § Civil and political rights](23438021.md#Civil_and_political_rights)



Arbitrary detentions in Venezuela between 2014 and 2019 according to Foro Penal. Arrests by year in blue and total arrests in red.

Repression and politically motivated detentions have risen to record levels in 2019.[^cite_note-PoliticalDetentions-323] [Foro Penal](54003280.md) states that Venezuela has at least 900 political prisoners as of April 2019, with more arrests of people being held longer in poor conditions and on dubious charges. The human rights organization has documented more than 50 instances that include "sexual abuse, strangulation using plastic bags and the use of razor blades to cut detainees' feet".[^cite_note-PoliticalDetentions-323] In the first three months of 2019, Foro Penal says 1,712 people were arrested and about two-thirds of those were held for more than 48 hours, the threshold used to classify a detainee as a political prisoner.[^cite_note-PoliticalDetentions-323] Maduro calls those arrested members of "terrorist groups" and says his government will not hesitate to send them to prison. [Juan Requesens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Requesens) and [Roberto Marrero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roberto_Marrero) are examples of "purely political" arrests, according to their attorney.[^cite_note-PoliticalDetentions-323] Increasingly high numbers of the detainees are working-class people, who have been driven to protest by the crisis.[^cite_note-PoliticalDetentions-323]

The final published report addressed the extrajudicial executions, torture, [forced disappearances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_disappearance) and other human rights violations reportedly committed by Venezuelan security forces in the recent years.[^cite_note-reutersIndependenceday-324] Bachelet expressed her concerns for the "shockingly high" number of extrajudicial killings and urged for the dissolution of the FAES.[^cite_note-Fr24Michellereport-325] According to the report, 1,569 cases of executions as consequence as a result of "resistance to authority" were registered by the Venezuelan authorities from 1 January to 19 March.[^cite_note-Fr24Michellereport-325] Other 52 deaths that occurred during 2019 protests were attributed to colectivos.[^cite_note-326] The report also details how the Venezuelan government "aimed at neutralising, repressing and criminalising political opponents and people critical of the government" since 2016.[^cite_note-Fr24Michellereport-325]

On 16 September 2020, the United Nations Fact Finding Mission on Venezuela accused the Maduro government of [crimes against humanity](61099.md).[^cite_note-327]

On 15 February 2024, the Maduro government closed the [UN High Commissioner for Human Rights](22463.md) office in Caracas after High Commissioner [Volker Türk](71721726.md) condemned the detention of activist [Rocío San Miguel](61099564.md), demanding "her immediate release and respect for her right to legal defense". Maduro's government expelled its officials, giving them 72 hours to leave the country.[^cite_note-328]

### Emigration

Further information: [Venezuelan refugee crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_refugee_crisis)



[National Police of Colombia](7278792.md) accompanying Venezuelans to [Cúcuta](623811.md)

The exodus of millions of desperate impoverished Venezuelans to surrounding countries has been called "a risk for the entire region".[^cite_note-Larmer-1-11-18-60] Millions of [Venezuelan people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_people) have voluntarily emigrated from Venezuela during the Chávez and Maduro presidencies.[^cite_note-EUdiaspora-329][^cite_note-NEWSWEEKdiaspora-330] The crisis started during the Chávez presidency, but became much more pronounced during Maduro's term.[^cite_note-MigrationCrisis-331] Emigration has been motivated by economic collapse, expansion of [state control over the economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statist_socialist), high crime, high inflation, general uncertainty, a lack of hope for a change in government,[^cite_note-EUdiaspora-329][^cite_note-WSJ2011-332] a failing public sector, and "shortages of basic necessities".[^cite_note-MigrationCrisis-331] The PGA Group estimates more than 1.5 million Venezuelans emigrated in the 15 years between 1999 and 2014;[^cite_note-EUdiaspora-329] an estimated 1.8 million left in ten years through 2015.[^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334]

The UN said that in the first part of 2018, about 5,000 Venezuelans were leaving Venezuela daily.[^cite_note-UNHCR-MIO2019-335] A February 2019 UN report estimated that 3.4 million Venezuelans have emigrated, and they expect another 1.9 million may emigrate in 2019.[^cite_note-Reuters29Mar-206][^cite_note-UNHCR-MIO2019-335] The UN estimates 2.7 million have gone to the Caribbean and Latin America.[^cite_note-NPR4April-336][^cite_note-UNHCR-MIO2019-335] Most have gone to Colombia; estimates of Venezuelans emigrating to Colombia are 1.1 million, Peru 506,000, Chile 288,000, Ecuador 221,000, Argentina 130,000, and Brazil 96,000.[^cite_note-NPR4April-336] This is in contrast to Venezuela's high immigration rate during the 20th century.[^cite_note-NEWSWEEKdiaspora-330] Kevin Whitaker, the U.S. ambassador in Colombia, says, "Colombians, in their tens and hundreds of thousands, migrated to Venezuela in the '60s and '70s and '80s, when Venezuela was a wealthy country and Colombia was not so much. Now, more than 1 million Venezuelans, many of them since 2015, have gone to live in Colombia."[^cite_note-NPR4April-336]

Those who leave by foot are known as *los caminantes* (the walkers); the walk to Bogotá, Colombia is 560 kilometres (350 mi), and some walk hundreds of kilometres further, to Ecuador or Peru.[^cite_note-NPR4April-336] Alba Pereira, who helps feed and clothe about 800 walkers daily in Northern Colombia, said in 2019 she is seeing more sick, elderly and pregnant among the walkers.[^cite_note-NPR4April-336] The Colombian [Red Cross](15487.md) has set up rest tents with food and water on the side of the roads for Venezuelans.[^cite_note-Walkers-337] Venezuelans also cross into northern Brazil, where UNHCR has set up 10 shelters to house thousands of Venezuelans.[^cite_note-Walkers-337] Images of Venezuelans fleeing the country by sea have raised symbolic comparisons to the images seen from the [Cuban diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_diaspora).[^cite_note-NYThungryDIA-338]

In 1998, only 14 Venezuelans were granted U.S. asylum, and by September 1999, 1,086 Venezuelans were granted asylum according to the [U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Citizenship_and_Immigration_Services).[^cite_note-Reuters2007-339] The first wave of Venezuelan emigrants were wealthy and middle class Venezuelans concerned by Chávez's rhetoric of redistributing wealth to the poor;[^cite_note-NYThungryDIA-338] the early exodus of college-educated people with capital caused a [brain drain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_capital_flight).[^cite_note-MigrationCrisis-331]

Emigration especially increased during the Maduro presidency.[^cite_note-REUT15oct-340] This second wave of emigration consisted of lower class Venezuelans suffering directly from the economic crisis facing the country; thus, the same individuals whom Chávez attempted to aid were now seeking to emigrate, driven by worsening economic conditions, scarcity of food and medicine, and rising rates of violent crime.[^cite_note-NYThungryDIA-338] Tomás Pérez, who studies the Venezuelan diaspora at the Central University of Venezuela, said in 2018 that because "now everyone is poor", it is mostly poor leaving the country.[^cite_note-MigrationCrisis-331] Carlos Malamud, from a Spanish think tank, said Maduro is "using migration as a political weapon against the opposition".[^cite_note-MigrationCrisis-331] The scale of the crisis has surpassed in four years the Cuban exodus, in which 1.7 million emigrated over a period of sixty years; Malamud says "Latin American societies aren't prepared for such wide-scale arrivals".[^cite_note-MigrationCrisis-331]

Impacting the health care crisis in Venezuela, health care professionals are emigrating; a primary factor driving emigration to Colombia is the lack of "medicines, supplies, health providers, and basic health services" in Venezuela.[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207] Since 2017, the banking sector has seen 18,000 employees leave the country.[^cite_note-341]

#### Migration crisis

The [Organization of American States](22788.md) (OAS) and spokespersons for the [United Nations refugee agency](31964.md), UNHCR, have described it as the largest exodus in the history of the Western Hemisphere in the last 50 years.[^cite_note-342][^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344]

## Economic

Further information: [2013–present economic crisis in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013%E2%80%93present_economic_crisis_in_Venezuela) and [Economic policy of the Nicolás Maduro administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_administration)

Maduro's government stopped releasing social and economic indicators, so most data rely on estimates.[^cite_note-9Charts-205] The [Institute of International Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_International_Finance) (IIF) stated in March 2019 that "Venezuela's economic collapse is among the world's worst in recent history".[^cite_note-9Charts-205] A chief economist of the IIF said the crisis resulted from "policy decisions, economic mismanagement, and political turmoil", saying it is on a scale that "one would only expect from extreme natural disasters or military confrontations".[^cite_note-9Charts-205] The April 2019 [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) (IMF) World Economic Outlook described Venezuela as being in a "wartime economy".[^cite_note-WartimeEconomy3-62] For the fifth consecutive year, Bloomberg rated Venezuela last on its [misery index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misery_index_\(economics\)) in 2019.[^cite_note-345] The government's main source of income is oil, with output "plummeting due to lack of investment, poor maintenance and neglect",[^cite_note-9Charts-205] from which consultant Eduardo Fortuny expects will take 12 years to recover.[^cite_note-9Charts-205]

As of 2020 the Venezuelan government has liberalized many socialist or redistributive economic policies—price and currency controls, stringent labor laws—and brought a [rapprochement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapprochement) with members of the local business community—especially [Lorenzo Mendoza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lorenzo_Mendoza) of the iconic [Empresas Polar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empresas_Polar) conglomerate (who is no longer denounced as a "thief," a "parasite" and a "traitor"), in exchange for an abandonment of political opposition by Mendoza.[^cite_note-Kurmanaev-2020-346] However, a "slight recovery" in economic activity in January 2020 reportedly "evaporated in February and March" due to "the fall in global oil prices and the coronavirus pandemic".[^cite_note-Reuters-25-Nov-2020-347]

### Business and industry

See also: [Perrera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perrera)



Ratings for Venezuela from 1998 to 2017 by the U.S. Government-funded NGO [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House)

[^cite_note-348]

&#x20;(1 = free, 7 = not free)

A number of foreign firms have left the nation—often due to quarrels with the socialist government—including [Smurfit Kappa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smurfit_Kappa), [Clorox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clorox), [Kimberly Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimberly_Clark) and [General Mills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Mills); the departures aggravate unemployment and shortages.[^cite_note-CompaniesFlee-349] Before the effects of the 2019 Venezuelan blackouts, the number of multinational companies in the industrial city of [Valencia in Carabobo State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valencia,_Carabobo) had dropped from 5,000 when Chávez became president to a tenth of that.[^cite_note-350]

#### Airline industry

Domestic airlines are having difficulties because of hyperinflation and parts shortages, and most [international airlines have left the country](8277069.md#Chronology_of_former_international_flights).[^cite_note-AirlineSuffer-351][^cite_note-ArgAir-352] Airlines from many countries ceased operating in Venezuela, making travel to the country difficult:[^cite_note-ArgAir-352] [Air Canada](145623.md) became the first international airline to cease Venezuela operations in March 2014 and was followed by [Alitalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alitalia) in April 2015.[^cite_note-353]

Other airlines that have left are [Aeroméxico](84578.md), [Avianca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avianca), [Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_Air_Lines), [Lufthansa](97733.md), [LATAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LATAM_Airlines_Group), and [United Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Airlines).[^cite_note-DeltaCuts-354] According to the [International Air Transport Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Air_Transport_Association) (IATA), the government of Venezuela has not paid US\$3.8 billion to international airlines in an issue involving conversion of local currency to U.S. dollars.[^cite_note-DeltaCuts-354] Airlines have left for other reasons, including crime against flight crews and foreign passengers, stolen baggage, and problems with the quality of jet fuel and maintenance of runways.[^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357] [Aerolíneas Argentinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerol%C3%ADneas_Argentinas) left in 2017, citing security reasons,[^cite_note-ArgAir-352] and [American Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Airlines), the last U.S. airline serving Venezuela, left on 15 March 2019, after its pilots refused to fly to Venezuela, citing safety issues.[^cite_note-358] Currently, the only North American airline flying to Venezuela is [Sunwing Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunwing_Airlines), with seasonal service to [Margarita Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margarita_Island) and [Punto Fijo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punto_Fijo).

Following the increasing economic partnership between Venezuela and Turkey in October 2016, [Turkish Airlines](167925.md) started offering direct flights from December 2016 connecting between Caracas to Istanbul (via Havana, Cuba) in an effort to "link and expand contacts" between the two countries.[^cite_note-359]

Iranian airline [Mahan Air](375904.md) (blacklisted by the U.S. government since 2011[^cite_note-MahanBlacklist-360]) began direct flights to Caracas in April 2019,[^cite_note-361] "signifying a growing relationship between the two nations" according to [Fox News](11121.md).[^cite_note-MahanBlacklist-360]

In May 2019, the [United States Department of Transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Transportation) and [Department of Homeland Security](58236.md) suspended all flights between Venezuela and the United States, due to safety and security concerns.[^cite_note-362] The suspension affects mainly Venezuelan airlines flying to [Miami](128968.md), which are [Avior Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avior_Airlines), [LASER Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LASER_Airlines) and [Estelar Latinoamérica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estelar_Latinoam%C3%A9rica).

### Gross domestic product

Estimated to drop by 25% in 2019, the IMF said the contraction in Venezuela's GDP—the largest since the [Libyan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Civil_War) began in 2014—was affecting all of Latin America.[^cite_note-WartimeEconomy3-62]

In 2015 the Venezuelan economy contracted 5.7% and in 2016 it contracted 18.6% according to the Venezuelan central bank;[^cite_note-Reuters2016Inflation-145] after that, the government stopped producing data.[^cite_note-9Charts-205] Ecoanalítica, a Venezuelan consultant, told *The Wall Street Journal* that output had halved between 2016 and 2019.[^cite_note-9Charts-205] The IMF and AGPV Asesores Económicos, a consulting firm based in Caracas, estimate that GDP shrunk to \$80 billion in 2018 from \$196 billion in 2013, making the economy smaller than Guatemala's or Ethiopia's.[^cite_note-9Charts-205]

### Inflation

Main article: [Hyperinflation in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperinflation_in_Venezuela)



Venezuela hyperinflation (May 2018 – October 2019)

The annual inflation rate for consumer prices has risen hundreds of thousands of percent during the crisis.[^cite_note-Larmer-1-11-18-60] Inflation in Venezuela remained high during Chávez's presidency. By 2010, inflation removed any advancement of wage increases,[^cite_note-363] and by 2014 at 69%[^cite_note-364] it was the highest in the world.[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366] In November 2016, Venezuela entered a period of [hyperinflation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperinflation),[^cite_note-367] with inflation reaching 4,000% in 2017;[^cite_note-WeightLoss-215] the Venezuelan government "essentially stopped" producing inflation estimates in early 2018.[^cite_note-NYRoB-20183-63] At the end of 2018, inflation had reached 1.35 million percent.[^cite_note-MaduroStarts-368]

In the 2017 Christmas season, some shops stopped using price tags since prices would inflate so quickly.[^cite_note-369] From 2017 to 2019, some Venezuelans became video game [gold farmers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold_farmer) and could be seen playing games such as *[RuneScape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RuneScape)* to sell in-game currency or characters for real currency; players could make more money than salaried workers by earning only a few dollars per day.[^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371] Some of these "gold farmers" will use cryptocurrencies as an intermediary currency before converting into Bolivares, as [indicated in this interview](http://bitcoinmxn.com/2018/09/01/una-entrevista-con-un-anonimo-venezolano-utilizando-runescape-y-bitcoin-para-alimentar-a-su-familia/).

In October 2018, the IMF estimated that inflation would reach 10,000,000% by the end of 2019.[^cite_note-372]

In early 2019, the monthly minimum salary was the equivalent of US\$5.50 (18,000 sovereign bolivars)—less than the price of a Happy Meal at McDonald's.[^cite_note-WartimeEconomy3-62] Ecoanalitica estimated that prices jumped by 465% in the first two-and-a-half months of 2019.[^cite_note-9Charts-205] *The Wall Street Journal* stated in March 2019 that the "main cause of hyperinflation is the central bank printing money to increase money supply, thus boosting domestic spending.", reporting that a teacher can buy a dozen eggs and two pounds of cheese with a month's wages.[^cite_note-9Charts-205]

In May 2019, the Central Bank of Venezuela released economic data for the first time since 2015. According to the release, Venezuela's inflation rate was 274% in 2016, 863% in 2017 and 130,060% in 2018.[^cite_note-373] The new reports imply a contraction of more than half of the economy in five years, according to the *Financial Times* "one of the biggest contractions in Latin American history".[^cite_note-374] Sources quoted by *Reuters,* said that China may have asked Venezuela to release the data to bring Venezuela into compliance with the IMF and make it more difficult for the IMF to recognise Juan Guaidó during the presidential crisis.[^cite_note-reutersIMFdeniesBCV-375] The IMF said it was not able to assess the quality of the data as it had no contact with the Venezuelan government.[^cite_note-reutersIMFdeniesBCV-375]

### Shortages

Further information: [Shortages in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_in_Venezuela)

Shortages in Venezuela became prevalent after [price controls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Price_controls) were enacted according to the economic policy of the Hugo Chávez government.[^cite_note-economist.com-137][^cite_note-qz.com-136] Under the [economic policy of the Nicolás Maduro government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_policy_of_the_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_government), greater shortages occurred due to the Venezuelan government's policy of withholding United States dollars from importers with price controls.[^cite_note-WSJseptDOWNGRADE-376] Some Venezuelans must search for food—occasionally resorting to eating wild fruit or garbage—wait in lines for hours and sometimes settle without having certain products.[^cite_note-APGarbage-211]

### Unemployment

"Wartime economy"

> \[Venezuela's wartime economy has the] world's highest unemployment since end of [Bosnian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_War) in world's biggest contraction since 2014 start of [Libyan Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libyan_Civil_War)

In January 2016 the unemployment rate was 18.1 percent[^cite_note-imf-datamapper-377] and the economy was the worst in the world according to the misery index.[^cite_note-economia.uol-378] Venezuela has not reported official unemployment figures since April 2016, when the rate was at 7.3 percent.[^cite_note-379]

Unemployment was forecasted to reach 44% for 2019; the IMF stated that this was the highest unemployment seen since the end of the Bosnian War in 1995.[^cite_note-WartimeEconomy3-62]

### Venezuelan debt

In August 2017, [President of the United States](24113.md) [Donald Trump](4848272.md) imposed sanctions on Venezuela[^cite_note-380] which banned transactions involving Venezuela's state debt including debt restructuring. The technical default period ended 13 November 2017 and Venezuela did not pay coupons on its dollar eurobonds, causing a cross-default on other dollar bonds. A committee consisting of the fifteen largest banks admitted default on state debt obligations which in turn entailed payments on CDS on 30 November.[^cite_note-381]

In November 2017, *The Economist* estimated Venezuela's debt at US\$105 billion and its reserves at US\$10 billion.[^cite_note-382] In 2018, Venezuela's debt grew to US\$156 billion[^cite_note-383] and as of March 2019, its reserves had dropped to US\$8 billion.[^cite_note-PayConoco-384]

With the exception of PDVSA's 2020 bonds,[^cite_note-385] as of January 2019, all of Venezuela's bonds are in default,[^cite_note-386] and Venezuela's government and state-owned companies owe nearly US\$8 billion in unpaid interest and principal.[^cite_note-387] As of March 2019, the government and state-owned companies have US\$150 billion in debt.[^cite_note-PayConoco-384]

### Oil industry

By 2018 the political and economic troubles facing Venezuela had engulfed the [El Tigre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Tigre)-[San Tomé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Tom%C3%A9,_Venezuela) region, a key region for oil production in eastern Venezuela. Oil workers were fleeing the state-owned oil company as salaries could not keep up with hyperinflation, reducing families to starvation. Workers and criminals stripped vital oil industry equipment of anything valuable, ranging from pickup trucks to the copper wire within critical oil production components.[^cite_note-workers_flee-388] Oil facilities were neglected and unprotected, crippling oil production and leading to [environmental damage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_Venezuela).[^cite_note-Leaking-389] As noted petroleum historian, expert, and former San Tomé resident [Emma Brossard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emma_Brossard)[^cite_note-Brossard1993-390] stated in 2005, "Venezuelan oil fields had a depletion rate of 25 per cent annually \[and] there had to be an investment of US\$3.4 billion a year to keep up its production." "But since Chávez has become president there has been no investment."[^cite_note-Brossard05-391]

As of 2020 there were no longer any oil rigs searching for oil in Venezuela, and production has been "reduced to a trickle". Oil exports are expected to total \$2.3 billion for 2020, continuing a decline of more than a decade. Pollution from crude oil leaking from abandoned underwater wells and pipelines has caused serious damage to fishing and human health.[^cite_note-Urdaneta-nyt-392]

In 2022, rising oil prices caused by the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) led the World Oil Commission to start meetings with the Venezuelan Government to push oil production to have a control over the price. As a consequence of the energy crisis caused by the war, the [United States](195149.md) allowed the American oil and gas company [Chevron](284749.md) to resume limited operations in Venezuela again.[^cite_note-cfr.org-257]

## Foreign involvement

### Economic sanctions

Main article: [Sanctions during the Venezuelan crisis](60408367.md)

Targeted sanctions against Venezuelan individuals and businesses began as early as 2005, over what the United States said were insufficient efforts by Venezuela to cooperate with its drug enforcement and anti-terrorism efforts.[^cite_note-393] In 2011, the United States sanctioned Venezuela's state-owned oil company [Petróleos de Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PDVSA).[^cite_note-BBC_News-2011-394] On 9 March 2015, [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) signed and issued an executive order declaring Venezuela a national security threat and ordered sanctions against Venezuelan officials. The sanctions did not affect Venezuela's oil company and trade relations with the US continued.[^cite_note-395] In 2017, Trump's administration imposed additional [economic sanctions](411315.md) on Venezuela.[^cite_note-396] The new sanctions prohibited Venezuela from borrowing from US financial institutions.[^cite_note-Weisbrot-2019-71]<sup>: 7 </sup> The European Union, the Lima Group, the United States and other countries have applied individual sanctions against government officials and members of both the military and security forces.[^cite_note-sanctions2-69] The United States would later extend its sanctions to the petroleum sector.[^cite_note-yahoo290119-397][^cite_note-cfr.org-257] According to *The Wall Street Journal*, the new 2019 sanctions aimed at depriving the Maduro government of petroleum revenues.[^cite_note-VenFear-398] During the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), world leaders called for a suspension of economic sanctions, including against Venezuela and Iran, that have "increasingly become the pursuit of war by other means". The US responded by intensifying the sanctions against Venezuela.[^cite_note-399] In June 2021, US banks blocked Venezuela's payments for COVID-19 vaccinations through the Covax program,[^cite_note-400] delaying the first shipment of the vaccines until September.[^cite_note-401]

#### Impact

According to executives within the company as well as the Venezuelan government, the 2011 United States sanctions were mostly symbolic and had little effect (if any) on Venezuela's trade with the US since the company's sale of oil to the US and the operations of its US-based subsidiary Citgo were unaffected.[^cite_note-BBC_News-2011-394]

The 2017 US sanctions prevented Venezuela from restructuring PDVSA's debt, because it could not exchange its existing debts for newly issued bonds. Because debt restructuring "is negotiated with groups of bondholders, which would invariably include US bondholders" and because non-US financial institutions feared retaliation from the US, the impact of this restriction went beyond the US financial institutions officially covered by the sanctions.[^cite_note-Weisbrot-2019-71]<sup>: 7 </sup> The 2017 sanctions also prevented Citgo from repatriating its profits to Venezuela, blocking the country from receiving billions in dividends.[^cite_note-402]

Sachs and Weisbrot argue that the 40,000 excess deaths measured between 2017 and 2018 are largely attributable to the impact of the 2017 US sanctions.[^cite_note-Weisbrot-2019-71]<sup>: 15 </sup> The report was criticized by other economists, who said the impact of the sanctions could not be adequately separated from pre-existing negative trends when determining excess deaths.[^cite_note-Hausmann\:0-72][^cite_note-\:1-73] Sachs and Weisbrot concede that the outcome in a counterfactual scenario without sanctions in unknowable, but argue that the sanctions "lock Venezuela into a downward economic spiral" by making policies that would ordinarily resolve a similar crisis impossible to implement.[^cite_note-Weisbrot-2019-71]<sup>: 21 </sup>Sachs and Weisbrot analyze past instances of hyperinflation in Latin America and note that in historical cases, hyperinflation was brought under control using Exchange Rate Based Stabilization (ERBS) – "creating a new exchange rate system with full convertibility and a managed float of the currency." They argue that sanctions made pursuing an ERBS program impossible "because a peg to the dollar would require access to the dollar-based financial system, which the sanctions have cut off as much as possible." They also argue that sanctions made it impossible for Venezuela to securitize its oil reserves as collateral for a debt restructuring. They conclude that "the counterfactual possibility in the absence of sanctions is not just zero excess deaths, but actually a reduction in mortality and other improvements in health indicators. That is because an economic recovery could have already begun in the absence of economic sanctions."[^cite_note-Weisbrot-2019-71]<sup>: 19-21 </sup>

Michelle Bachelet updated the situation in a 20 March oral report following the visit of a five-person delegation to Venezuela, saying that the social and economic crisis was dramatically deteriorating, the government had not acknowledged or addressed the extent of the crisis, and she was concerned that although the "pervasive and devastating economic and social crisis began before the imposition of the first economic sanctions", the sanctions could worsen the situation.[^cite_note-403]

An October 2020 report published by the Washington Office on Latin America (WOLA) by Venezuelan economist Luis Oliveros found that "while Venezuela's economic crisis began before the first U.S. sectoral sanctions were imposed in 2017, these measures 'directly contributed to its deep decline, and to the further deterioration of the quality of life of Venezuelans' ". The report concluded that economic sanctions "have cost Venezuela's government as much as \$31 billion since 2017"[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405]

#### Legality

In 2019, former UN rapporteur [Alfred de Zayas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_de_Zayas) said that US sanctions on Venezuela were illegal as they constituted [economic warfare](4757142.md) and "could amount to 'crimes against humanity' under international law".[^cite_note-indreport-406] In February 2019, Jorge Arreaza, Maduro's Minister for Foreign Affairs, said he was forming a coalition of diplomats who "believe the U.S. and others are violating the U.N. charter against non-interference in member states". During the announcement, he was surrounded by diplomats from 16 other countries, including Russia, China, Iran, North Korea, and Cuba. Arreaza said the cost to the Venezuelan economy of the US blockade was over \$30 billion.[^cite_note-FMWarns-407] Reporting on Arreaza's statements, the *Associated Press* said that Maduro was blocking aid, and "saying that Venezuelans are not beggars and that the move is part of a U.S.-led coup".[^cite_note-408] [Alena Douhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alena_Douhan), [United Nations](31769.md) [Special Rapporteur on the negative impact of unilateral coercive measures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Rapporteur_on_the_negative_impact_of_unilateral_coercive_measures), was due to visit Venezuela in August 2020 to investigate the impact of [international sanctions](60408367.md).[^cite_note-Reuters_20202-409] Before her visit, 66 Venezuelan NGOs (including [PROVEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PROVEA)) asked Douhan in an open letter to consider the harmful impact of sanctions in the context of years of repression, corruption and economic mismanagement that predate the sanctions, and requested she meet independent press and civil society researchers.[^cite_note-Calles_Calles2-410][^cite_note-Lusa_20212-411][^cite_note-412][^cite_note-Käufer_20212-413] She arrived on 31 January, and was welcomed on arrival by a government minister and the Venezuelan ambassador to the UN.[^cite_note-Lusa_20212-411] She declared on her preliminary findings as she left on 12 February:[^cite_note-CNN_20212-414] that sanctions against Venezuela have had a "devastating" noticeable impact in both the economy and the population.[^cite_note-415] She said "the increasing number of unilateral sanctions imposed by United States, the European Union and other countries have exacerbated the economic and humanitarian calamities in Venezuela" but that Venezuela's economic decline "began in 2014 with the fall in oil prices" and that "mismanagement and corruption had also contributed." The government welcomed the report, while the opposition accused her of "playing into the hands of the regime" of Maduro.[^cite_note-Le_Monde.fr_20212-416][^cite_note-DW\.COM2-417][^cite_note-Sequera_20212-418][^cite_note-Moleiro_20212-419] Douhan was harshly criticized by the Venezuelan civil society,[^cite_note-420] and several non-governmental organizations pronounced themselves in social media with the hashtag "*#Lacrisisfueprimero*" (The crisis came first).[^cite_note-421][^cite_note-422][^cite_note-423]

#### Responsibility for the crisis

Some US-based economists have argued that the significance of US sanctions on Venezuela is minimal because shortages and high inflation in Venezuela began before US sanctions were directed towards the country.[^cite_note-424] *The Wall Street Journal* said that economists place the blame for Venezuela's economy shrinking by half on "Maduro's policies, including widespread nationalizations, out-of-control spending that sparked inflation, price controls that led to shortages, and widespread graft and mismanagement."[^cite_note-VenFear-398] The Venezuelan government has stated that the United States is responsible for its economic collapse.[^cite_note-VenFear-398] The HRW/Johns Hopkins report noted that most sanctions are "limited to canceling visas and freezing assets of key officials implicated in abuses and corruption. They in no way target the Venezuelan economy."[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207] The report also stated that the 2017 ban on dealing in Venezuelan government stocks and bonds allows exceptions for food and medicine, and that the 28 January 2019 PDVSA sanctions could worsen the situation, although "the crisis precedes them".[^cite_note-HRW2019Report-207] *The Washington Post* stated that "the deprivation long predates recently imposed US sanctions".[^cite_note-WAPOHRW-240]

### Military involvement

Main article: [Foreign involvement in the Venezuelan presidential crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_involvement_in_the_Venezuelan_presidential_crisis)



At a 18 February rally in [Doral, Florida](109422.md), President Trump urged Venezuela's military to abandon Nicolás Maduro or 'lose everything'.

[^cite_note-425]

On 11 August 2017, President Trump said that he is "not going to rule out a military option" to confront the autocratic government of Nicolás Maduro and the deepening crisis in Venezuela.[^cite_note-426] *Military Times* said the unnamed aides told Trump it was not wise to even discuss a military solution due to the [long history of unpopular intervention in Latin America by the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_America-United_States_relations).[^cite_note-MP7517-427] Venezuela's defense minister [Vladimir Padrino](45272341.md) criticized Trump for the statement, calling it "an act of supreme extremism" and "an act of madness". The Venezuelan communications minister, [Ernesto Villegas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernesto_Villegas), said Trump's words amounted to "an unprecedented threat to national sovereignty".[^cite_note-428]

Representatives of the United States were in contact with dissident Venezuelan military officers during 2017 and 2018 but declined to collaborate with them or provide assistance to them.[^cite_note-NYT9818-429] The opinion of other Latin American nations was split with respect to military intervention. [Luis Almagro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Almagro), the [Secretary General of the Organization of American States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_General_of_the_Organization_of_American_States), while visiting Colombia, did not rule out the potential benefit of the use of military force to intervene with the crisis. Canada, Colombia and Guyana, which are members of the Lima Group, refused to sign the organization's document rejecting military intervention in Venezuela.[^cite_note-CR91618-430][^cite_note-CBC91918-431]

During the 2019 Venezuelan presidential crisis, allegations of potential United States military involvement began to circulate,[^cite_note-Invasion-432] with military intervention in Venezuela was already being executed by the governments of Cuba and Russia.[^cite_note-Invasion-432] According to professor Erick Langer of [Georgetown University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University), while it was being discussed whether the United States would militarily intervene, "Cuba and Russia have already intervened".[^cite_note-Invasion-432] Hundreds or thousands of Cuban security forces have allegedly been operating in Venezuela while professor Robert Ellis of [United States Army War College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Army_War_College) described the between several dozen and 400 [Wagner Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wagner_Group) mercenaries provided by Russia as the "palace guard of Nicolás Maduro".[^cite_note-Invasion-432] Kremlin spokesman [Dmitry Peskov](9214683.md) denied the deployment of Russian mercenaries, calling it "fake news".[^cite_note-433] On 2 April 2019, the [Russian Foreign Ministry](4432213.md) rejected Trump's call to "get out" saying their 100 military servicemen now in Venezuela will support Maduro "for as long as needed".[^cite_note-434] In May 2020, Venezuelan dissidents backed by [Silvercorp USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_Goudreau#Silvercorp_USA) attempted to infiltrate Venezuela by sea and overthrow Maduro in [Operation Gideon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Gideon_\(2020\)). The raid failed, and the dissidents and mercenaries were killed or captured.[^cite_note-435] The owner of Silvercorp, an ex-US special operations sergeant, said he met with Trump administration officials about the plan.[^cite_note-436]

### Humanitarian aid

See also: [2019 shipping of humanitarian aid to Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_shipping_of_humanitarian_aid_to_Venezuela)



Aid for Venezuela sent by the United States to Colombia



Location of the humanitarian aid points outside of Venezuela

Throughout the crisis, humanitarian aid was provided to Venezuelans in need both within Venezuela and abroad. In October 2018, the [USNS *Comfort*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USNS_Comfort_\(T-AH-20\)) departed for an eleven-week operation in Latin America, with a primary mission being to assist countries who received [Venezuelan refugees who fled the crisis in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_refugee_crisis). The main goal was to relieve health systems in Colombia, Ecuador, Peru and other nations which faced the arrival of thousands of Venezuelan migrants.[^cite_note-437]

At the end of January 2019, as the US prepared to bring aid across the border, the [International Committee of the Red Cross](5023832.md) warned the United States about the risk of delivering humanitarian aid without the approval of the government's security forces.[^cite_note-PBS_NewsHour_2019_IRCR-438] The UN similarly warned the US about politicising the crisis and using aid as a pawn in the power struggle.[^cite_note-Nichols_2019-439] Other humanitarian organisations also raised risks.[^cite_note-NPR.org_2019_risks-440]

On 23 February 2019, 14 trucks carrying 280 tons of humanitarian aid attempted to bring aid across the Simon Bolivar and Francisco de Paula Santander bridges from Colombia. There were clashes, with Venezuelan security forces reported to use tear gas attack in attempt to maintain a blockade of the border. Colombia said around 285 people were injured and at least two trucks set on fire.[^cite_note-DW\.COM_trucks2-441][^cite_note-Reuters_2019_trucks2-442] [CNN](62028.md) reported that the Venezuela government accused Guaidó supporters of burning the trucks and noted that "While a CNN team saw incendiary devices from police on the Venezuelan side of the border ignite the trucks, the network's journalists are unsure if the trucks were burned on purpose."[^cite_note-Cullinane_Castillo_20192-443] In March, *The New York Times* reported that footage showed that it was anti-Maduro protestors rather than Venezuelan security forces who were responsible for the burning trucks.[^cite_note-The_New_York_Times_2019_video2-444][^cite_note-The_New_York_Times_2019_footage2-445] *The New York Times* reported that the trucks had been set on fire by anti-Maduro protester who threw a Molotov cocktail that hit one of the trucks.[^cite_note-The_New_York_Times_2019_footage2-445] Colombian foreign minister [Carlos Holmes Trujillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Holmes_Trujillo) rejected the claims by *The New York Times* that the Colombian government manipulated the video of the burning of the aid truck, insisting that Nicolás Maduro was responsible.[^cite_note-446] Responding when asked about the claims in a [BBC](1139893.md) interview, Juan Guaidó stressed that its findings suggested only a possible theory, that it was the newspaper's point of view and that a total of three trucks were burned, while the footage focused on one.[^cite_note-447] Journalist Karla Salcedo Flores denounced state-run [Telesur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telesur_\(TV_channel\)) for plagiarism and the manipulation of her photos for propaganda purposes after the network claimed protesters poured gasoline on the trucks.[^cite_note-448] [Agence France-Presse](471981.md) published an investigation disproving Telesur's claims with the photos.[^cite_note-449] [Bellingcat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellingcat) reported that since the open source evidence examined for its investigation does not show the moment of ignition, it is not possible to make a definitive determination regarding the cause of the fire.[^cite_note-450]

Franceso Rocca, president of the [International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies](9720597.md), announced on 29 March 2019 that the Red Cross was preparing to bring humanitarian aid to the country to help ease both the chronic hunger and the medical crisis.[^cite_note-RedCrossReady-451] *The Guardian* reported that Maduro had "long denied the existence of a humanitarian crisis, and on 23 February blocked an effort led by Guaidó to bring aid into the country", and that the Red Cross had "brokered a deal" between the Maduro and Guaidó administrations "indicating a seldom-seen middle ground between the two men".[^cite_note-RedCrossBrokers-452]

The Red Cross aid shipments were expected to begin within a few weeks, and the first shipment would help about 650,000 people;[^cite_note-RedCrossBrokers-452] simultaneously, a leaked UN report estimated that seven million Venezuelans were likely in need of humanitarian assistance.[^cite_note-GuardianHRW-237] During what *The Wall Street Journal* called "Latin America's worst humanitarian crisis ever", the "operation would rival Red Cross relief efforts in war-torn Syria, signaling the depth of Venezuela's crisis."[^cite_note-AidEffort-149] Rocca said the efforts would focus first on hospitals, including state-run facilities, and said the Red Cross was open to the possibility of delivering aid products stored on the Venezuelan borders with Colombia and Brazil.[^cite_note-WarnsAgainst-247] He warned that the Red Cross would not accept any political interference,[^cite_note-WarnsAgainst-247] and said the effort must be "independent, neutral, impartial and unhindered".[^cite_note-RedCrossBrokers-452]

Maduro and Arreaza met with representative of Red Cross International on 9 April to discuss the aid effort.[^cite_note-453] *The Wall Street Journal* said that the acceptance of humanitarian shipments by Maduro was his first acknowledgement that Venezuela is "suffering from an economic collapse", adding that "until a few days ago, the government maintained there was no crisis and it didn't need outside help".[^cite_note-AidEffort-149] Guaidó said the acceptance of humanitarian aid was the "result of our pressure and insistence",[^cite_note-AidEffort-149] and called on Venezuelans to "stay vigilant to make sure incoming aid is not diverted for 'corrupt' purposes".[^cite_note-WarnsAgainst-247]

The first Red Cross delivery of supplies for hospitals arrived on 16 April, offering an encouraging sign that the Maduro administration would allow more aid to enter.[^cite_note-FirstShipment-454] Quoting Tamara Taraciuk—an expert at Human Rights Watch on Venezuela—who called the situation "a completely man-made crisis", *The New York Times* said the aid effort in Venezuela presented challenges regarding how to deliver aid in an "unprecedented political, economic and humanitarian crisis" that was "caused largely by the policies of a government intent on staying in power, rather than war or natural disaster". Armed pro-government paramilitaries fired weapons to disrupt the first Red Cross delivery, and officials associated with Maduro's party told the Red Cross to leave.[^cite_note-Grapples-455]

An April 2021 report by the inspector general at [United States Agency for International Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Agency_for_International_Development) found that the Trump administration had politicized the early 2019 humanitarian aid package and was motivated by [regime change](37258993.md) in Venezuela more so than ameliorating the humanitarian situation there.[^cite_note-456][^cite_note-457]

## Public opinion

A November 2016 Datincorp survey that asked Venezuelans living in urban areas which entity was responsible for the crisis, 59% blamed [chavismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chavismo) or the presidents (Chávez, 25%; Maduro 19%; Chavismo 15%) while others blamed the opposition (10%), entrepreneurs (4%) and the United States (2%).[^cite_note-458]

A September 2018 Meganálisis survey found that 85% of Venezuelans wanted Maduro to leave power immediately.[^cite_note-459] A November 2018 Datanálisis poll found that 54% of Venezuelans opposed a foreign military intervention to remove Maduro, while 35% supported an intervention. Instead, 63% supported a "negotiated settlement to remove Maduro".[^cite_note-460]

An 11–14 March 2019 survey of 1,100 people in 16 Venezuelan states and 32 cities by Meganálisis found that 89% of respondents wanted Maduro to leave the presidency.[^cite_note-461] A Datanálisis poll on 4 March 2019 found Maduro's approval rating at an all-time low of 14%.[^cite_note-462]

According to Datanálisis, in early 2019, 63% of Venezuelans believed that a change of government was possible. Fourteen months later, in May 2020, after the [Macuto Bay raid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macuto_Bay_raid), the percentage decreased to 20%.[^cite_note-463]

According to economists interviewed by *The New York Times*, the situation is by far the worst economic crisis in Venezuela's history, and is also the worst facing a country in peace time since the mid-20th century. The crisis is also more severe than that of the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression_in_the_United_States) during the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression), the [1985–1994 Brazilian economic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985%E2%80%931994_Brazilian_economic_crisis), or the 2008–2009 [hyperinflation in Zimbabwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperinflation_in_Zimbabwe).[^cite_note-Kurmanaev-2019-61] Other writers have also compared aspects of the crisis, such as unemployment and GDP contraction, to that of [Bosnia and Herzegovina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Bosnia_and_Herzegovina) after the 1992–1995 [Bosnian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_War), and those in [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Russian_financial_crisis), [Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Period) and [Albania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Albanian_civil_unrest) following the [collapse of the Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collapse_of_the_Eastern_Bloc) in 1989 and the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) in 1991.[^cite_note-WartimeEconomy3-62][^cite_note-NYRoB-20183-63]

Argentina and Peru elected leftists who, for various reasons, didn't refer to themselves as socialists – but certainly governed as such. [^cite_note-162]: &#x20;["Venezuela: Events of 2018"](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2019/country-chapters/venezuela). Human Rights Watch. 20 December 2018. Retrieved 4 February 2019. No independent government institutions remain today in Venezuela to act as a check on executive power. A series of measures by the Maduro and Chávez governments stacked the courts with judges who make no pretense of independence. The government has been repressing dissent through often-violent crackdowns on street protests, jailing opponents, and prosecuting civilians in military courts. It has also stripped power from the opposition-led legislature. ...In 2017, President Maduro convened a 'Constituent Assembly' by presidential decree, despite a constitutional requirement that a public referendum be held before any effort to rewrite the Constitution. The assembly is made up exclusively of government supporters chosen through an election that Smartmatic, a British company hired by the government to verify the results, called fraudulent. The Constituent Assembly has, in practice, replaced the opposition-led National Assembly as the country's legislative branch.

[^cite_note-163]: &#x20;["Venezuela: Events of 2017"](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2018/country-chapters/venezuela). Human Rights Watch. 5 January 2018. Retrieved 4 February 2019. The Venezuelan government has jailed political opponents and disqualified them from running for office. At time of writing, more than 340 political prisoners were languishing in Venezuelan prisons or intelligence services headquarters, according to the Penal Forum, a Venezuelan network of pro-bono criminal defense lawyers. ...In mid-2017, the Supreme Court sentenced five opposition mayors, after summary proceedings that violated international norms of due process, to 15 months in prison and disqualified them from running for office.

[^cite_note-164]: &#x20;["Venezuela 2017–2018"](https://www.amnesty.org/en/countries/americas/venezuela/report-venezuela/). Amnesty International. Retrieved 4 February 2019. The judicial system continued to be used to silence dissidents, including using military jurisdiction to prosecute civilians. The justice system continued to be subject to government interference, especially in cases involving people critical of the government or those who were considered to be acting against the interests of the authorities. The Bolivarian National Intelligence Service continued to ignore court decisions to transfer and release people in its custody.

[^cite_note-165]: &#x20;["Wave of arrests as government turns against elected opposition"](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2017/08/venezuela-wave-of-arrests-as-government-turns-against-elected-opposition/). Amnesty International. 11 August 2017. Retrieved 4 February 2019. The arrest of four officials from the opposition in Venezuela, the removal from office of a further 11 and the issuing of arrest warrants against another five, demonstrates the Maduro administration's tightening stranglehold on any form of dissent, taking repression to a frightening new level, said Amnesty International.
